Heat Generation using Lorentzian Nanoparticles: Estimation via Time-Domain Techniques
Arpan Mukherjee, Mourad Sini

Abstract
We analyze the mathematical model that describes the heat generated by electromagnetic nanoparticles. We use the known optical properties of the nanoparticles to control the support and amount of the heat needed around a nanoparticle. Precisely, we show that the dominant part of the heat around the nanoparticle is the electric field multiplied by a constant dependent, explicitly and only, on the permittivity and quantities related to the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of the Magnetization (or the Newtonian) operator, defined on the nanoparticle, and inversely proportional to the distance to the nanoparticle. The nanoparticles are described via the Lorentz model. If the used incident frequency is chosen related to the plasmonic frequency ω p \omega_{p} (via the Magnetization operator) then the nanoparticle behaves as a plasmonic one while if it is chosen related to the undamped resonance frequency ω 0 \omega_{0} (via the Newtonian operator), then it behaves as a dielectric one. The two regimes exhibit different optical behaviors. In both cases, we estimate the generated heat and discuss advantages of each incident frequency regime. The analysis is based on time-domain integral equation techniques avoiding the use of (formal) Fourier type transformations.



Keywords:
Asymptotic Analysis; Boundary Integral Equations; Heat Equation; Helmholtz Equation; Layer and Volume Potentials, Dielectric and Plasmonic Resonances.

1
Problem Formulation and the Main Results

1.1
General introduction

Heat generation is used in many applications including medical imaging and therapy
[
1
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
12
,
23
,
30
]
. For instance the photo-acoustic imaging modality is based on using the acoustic pressure fluctuations, collected in an accessible part of the region to image, to recover few optical, and eventually acoustical, properties of the tissue. This pressure is generated by the heat created after exciting locally the tissue with electric laser fields
[
3
,
4
,
20
,
36
]
. This heat generation phenomenon has been also proposed as a thermal therapy to cure anomalies (as tumors) by injecting nanoparticles in the region occupied by the anomaly
[
2
,
4
]
. The main principle behind this phenomenon can be described as follows. It is known that an electric laser field excites surface plasmons, on metallic nanoparticles, at optical frequencies. In turn, these nanoparticles produce heat from the absorbed energy that diffuses away from them to raise the temperature of the surrounding medium.
In this work, we mainly focus on this therapy application. For this application, the question raised is how to control the generated heat so that it is enough to clean the anomaly but not so high to harm the surrounding tissue.

To describe the mathematical model behind this heat generation using electromagnetic nanoparticles, we split it into parts. In the first part, we describe electromagnetic wave propagation generated by injected nanoparticles and in the second one, we describe the related heat model.

Let us then recall the electromagnetic problem described by the time-dependent Maxwell equation

\begin{cases}\nabla\times\textbf{E}=-\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\textbf{H}\\[10.0pt]
\nabla\times\textbf{H}=\varepsilon\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\textbf{E},\end{cases}

(1.1)

where
E
and
H
are the total electric and magnetic field respectively. In addition, the coefficients
\mu
and
\varepsilon
are the magnetic permeability and electric permittivity, respectively.

We assume that the nanoparticle occupy a bounded domain
\Omega
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In addition, denote
\Omega=\delta B+\mathrm{z}
, where
\delta
denotes the size of the nanoparticle,
B
is centred at origin and
\mathrm{z}
represent the position of the nanoparticle.

Moreover, consider the electric permittivity and magnetic permeability, respectively, of the form
\varepsilon={\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\Omega}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}}

\mu=\mu_{\mathrm{p}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\Omega}+\mu_{\mathrm{m}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}}
. We denote by
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}=\varepsilon_{\infty}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\mathrm{m}}
to be the relative permittivity and
\mu_{\mathrm{m}}=\mu_{\infty}\mu^{\prime}_{\mathrm{m}}
the relative permeability of the host medium, respectively. Both are assumed to be constant and independent of the frequency
\omega
of the incident wave (i.e. the host medium is non dispersive). Here,
\varepsilon_{\infty}
and
\mu_{\infty}
are the electric permittivity and magnetic permeability of the free space respectively. Next, we assume the nanoparticle to be nonmagnetic, i.e.
\mu_{\mathrm{p}}=\mu_{\infty}\mu^{\prime}_{m}
. But its permittivity
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}
is given by the so-called Lorentz model which can be described as follows

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}(\omega,\gamma)=\varepsilon_{\infty}\Bigg[1+\dfrac{\omega_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}}{\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2}-i\gamma\omega}\Bigg]

(1.2)

where
\omega^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}
is the electric plasma frequency,
\omega^{2}_{0}
is the undamped resonance frequency and
\gamma
is the electric damping parameter, see Section
7.4
.

We deal with two types of nanoparticles:

1.

Plasmonic nanoparticles
. These are characterized by the conditions that
\bm{\Re}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})<0
and
\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})>0
and both of them have moderate amplitudes. The second condition represents the ability of the biological tissues to absorb electromagnetic radiation and hence create the heat while the first one is responsible for creating resonances (namely the plasmonic ones) that allow for controlling the heat.

2.

Dielectric nanoparticles
. These are characterized by the conditions

\bm{\Re}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})\gg 1
, and positive, and eventually
\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})\gg 1
but with a small ratio
\frac{\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{\bm{\Re}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}\ll 1
. Similarly as above, the second condition allows for heat generation while the second is responsible for creating the Dielectric resonances. With these resonances, we enhance the generated heat while with smallness of the above ratio, we control the upper bound of this heat.

As we can see it later, based on the Lorentz model (
1.2
), the nanoparticle behaves as a plasmonic or as a dielectric one according to the regimes we choose for the incident frequency
\omega
. Precisely, if the incident frequency
\omega
is chosen related to the the plasmonic frequency
\omega_{p}
then it behaves as a plasmonic one while if it is chosen related to the undamped frequency
\omega_{0}
then it behaves as a dielectric one. In both cases, one needs appropriate choices of the damping frequency
\gamma
(but with small values), see Section
7.4.1
and Section
7.4.2
, respectively, for more details.

1.2
The electromagnetic models

In this work, we mainly focus on the
2\mathrm{D}
model describing the time harmonic regime in TM-polarization and TE-polarization respectively.

1. First, in the TM-regime, we use incident waves of the form
\mathbb{E}^{in}:=e^{i\omega\sqrt{\mu_{m}\epsilon_{m}}(x\cdot\theta-t)}\;(1,1,0)
and then the total field of the form

\textbf{{E}}=\mathbb{E}\;e^{-i\omega t}
and
\textbf{H}=\mathbb{H}\;e^{-i\omega t}
, where
\mathbb{E}=(\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2}),0):=(\mathrm{E}_{1}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2}),\mathrm{E}_{2}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2}),0)
and
\mathbb{H}=\Big(0,0,\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})\Big)
. From (
1.1
), we obtain the following equation

\nabla\cdot\left[\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla\mathrm{H}\right]+\omega^{2}\mu_{m}\mathrm{H}=0\ \text{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{2}

(1.3)

where
\mathrm{H}=\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}}+\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{s}}
with
\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}}
as the incident wave and
\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{s}}
as the scattered wave satisfying the Sommerfield radiation condition
\lim_{|\mathrm{x}|\to\infty}|\mathrm{x}|\Big(\frac{\partial\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{s}}}{\partial|\mathrm{x}|}-\mathrm{i}\omega\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}}\ \mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{s}}\Big)=0
. The incident magnetic field satisfies the following equation
\Delta\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}}+\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}}=0\quad\text{in}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Additionally, the electric field
\mathrm{E}
can be calculated from the magnetic field using the following relation

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{x})=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\mathrm{x_{2}}}\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{x})\\
-\partial_{\mathrm{x_{1}}}\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{x})\end{pmatrix}.

(1.4)

Therefore, it can be readily seen that
|\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{x})|^{2}=|\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{x})|^{2}.

2. Next, in the TE-regime, we use incident waves of the form
\mathbb{E}^{in}:=e^{i\omega\sqrt{\mu_{m}\epsilon_{m}}(x\cdot\theta-t)}\;(0,0,1)
and then the total fields
\textbf{{E}}=\mathbb{E}\;e^{-i\omega t}
, where
\mathbb{E}=(0,0,\mathrm{E}_{3}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2}))
, satisfies the following equation

\displaystyle\Delta\mathrm{E}+\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\varepsilon\mathrm{E}=0\quad\text{in}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2},

(1.5)

and the corresponding scattered wave satisfies the Sommerfield radiation condition as
|\mathrm{x}|\to\infty
.

In the coming analysis, we use equation (
1.5
) to describe the electric field when it interacts with dielectric nanoparticles, while the model equation (
1.3
) will be used for plasmonic nanoparticles.

1.3
The heat generation model

As described above, laser electric fields can generate surface plasmons at certain ranges of frequencies so that the energy absorbed by them generates heat. The temperature at the surrounding medium created when the heat diffuses from the nanoparticle, is governed by the following model
[
2
]
,
[
4
]

\begin{cases}\rho\mathrm{c}\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}-\nabla.\ \gamma\nabla u=\frac{\omega}{2\pi}\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon)|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\ \ in\ \ (\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\partial\Omega)\times(0,\mathrm{T}),\\
\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}u\ -\ \gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}u=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ on\ \partial\Omega,\\
\gamma_{\mathrm{\mathrm{p}}}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}u\ -\ \gamma_{\mathrm{m}}\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}u=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ on\ \partial\Omega,\\
u(\mathrm{x},0)=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ \mathrm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{cases}

(1.6)

where
\rho=\rho_{\mathrm{p}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\Omega}+\rho_{m}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}}
is the mass density;
\mathrm{c}=\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{p}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\Omega}+\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{m}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}}
is the thermal capacity;
\gamma=\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\Omega}+\gamma_{m}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}}
is the thermal conductivity and we recall that
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\Omega}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}}
is the electric permittivity respectively. Here,
\mathrm{T}\in\mathbb{R}
is the final time of measurement.

In the sequel, we use the notations for both an interior Dirichlet and Neumann trace, whenever they make sense,

\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{u}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}):=\lim\limits_{\Omega\ni\tilde{\mathrm{x}}\to\mathrm{x}\in\partial\Omega}\mathrm{u}(\mathrm{\tilde{\mathrm{x}}},\mathrm{t})\ \text{for}\ (\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R},

and

\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{u}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}):=\lim\limits_{\Omega\ni\tilde{\mathrm{x}}\to\mathrm{x}\in\partial\Omega}\nu_{\mathrm{x}}\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{\mathrm{x}}}\mathrm{u}(\mathrm{\tilde{\mathrm{x}}},\mathrm{t})\ \text{for}\ (\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R},\ \text{respectively}.

We use similar notations for the exterior Dirichlet and Neumann traces
\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}\ \text{and}\ \gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}
respectively.

Furthermore, with
\mathrm{T}_{0}
fixed, considering the fact that
u=0
for
t<0
, we let
\mathrm{U}
to be the solution of the following problem, stated in the whole time domain
\mathbb{R}

\begin{cases}\rho\mathrm{c}\frac{\partial\mathrm{U}}{\partial t}-\nabla.\ \gamma\nabla\mathrm{U}=\frac{\omega}{2\pi}\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon)|\mathrm{E}|^{2}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{(0,T_{0})}\ \ in\ \ (\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\partial\Omega)\times\mathbb{R},\\
\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}\ -\ \gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}\mathrm{U}=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ on\ \partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R},\\
\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}\ -\ \gamma_{\mathrm{m}}\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}\mathrm{U}=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ on\ \partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}.\end{cases}

(1.7)

As we have
\mathrm{U}=u
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(-\infty,T_{0})
then to analyse
u\ \text{in}\ (0,T_{0})
, it is enough to study
\mathrm{U}.

We further assume, for the purpose of simplicity of the analysis, that
\rho_{\mathrm{p}},\rho_{m},\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{p}},\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{m}},\gamma_{\mathrm{p}},\gamma_{m}
are positive constants. We also note that the heat equation becomes a homogeneous equation outside of
\Omega
as
\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon)=0
in
(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega})
. Consequently, we can rewrite the governing heat equations and transmissions as follows

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{p}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\frac{\partial\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}}{\partial t}-\Delta\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}=\frac{\omega}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})|\mathrm{E}|^{2}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{(0,\mathrm{T_{0}})}\ \ in\ \ \Omega\times\mathbb{R}\\
\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{m}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{m}}}\frac{\partial\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}}{\partial t}-\Delta\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ in\ \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}\\
\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\ -\ \gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ on\ \partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R},\\
\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\ -\ \gamma_{m}\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ on\ \partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}.\end{cases}

(1.8)

For ease and clarity of notation, throughout this work we denote the diffusion constants by
\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}:=\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{p}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}
and
\alpha_{m}:=\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{m}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{m}}}
respectively.

We assume that the nanoparticle has the following scales regarding the heat-related coefficients

\gamma_{p}\sim\delta^{-2}~~\mbox{ and }~~\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{p}}\sim 1,~~\delta\ll 1.

(1.9)

More general scales could be considered, namely
\gamma_{p}\sim\delta^{-m}~~\mbox{ and }~~\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{p}}\sim\delta^{-n},~~\delta\ll 1
, with
m
and
n
non-negative such that
m-n-1\geq 0
.
1
1

1

This last condition ensures that the diffusion coefficient
\alpha_{p}
is small so that the, corresponding, last part of (
1.17
) in Theorem
1.3
is valid, see precisely (
4.16
).
To avoid introducing more parameters, we stick to the case
m=2,n=0
. Materials enjoying such scales can be found in the applied literature as
[
21
,
23
]
for instance.

1.4
Statement of the results

1.4.1
The electromagnetic field generated by plasmonic nanoparticles

We recall the fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation in dimension two given by

\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})=\dfrac{i}{4}\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{0}(\mathrm{k}|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|),\quad\mathrm{x}\neq\mathrm{y},

where
\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{0}
is the Hankel function of the first kind of order zero.
Moreover, we also introduce the Magnetic operator
\mathbb{M}:\nabla H_{arm}\rightarrow\nabla H_{arm},
defined as follows

\mathbb{M}\Big[\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big](\mathrm{x})=\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}.

(1.10)

We also need to introduce the following decomposition of the space
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)
into the following three sub-spaces as a direct sum as following, see
[
33
]
for more details

\mathbb{L}^{2}=\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{div},0)\oplus\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{curl},0)\oplus\nabla\mathrm{H}_{\textbf{arm}},

where we define these three sub-spaces as follows:

{}\begin{cases}\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{div},0)=\left\{u\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega):\nabla\cdot u=0\ \text{and}\ u\cdot\nu=0\right\},\\
\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{curl},0)=\left\{u\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega):\nabla\times u=0\ \text{and}\ u\times\nu=0\right\},\\
\nabla\mathrm{H}_{\textbf{arm}}=\left\{u\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega):\exists\ \varphi\ \text{s.t.}\ u=\nabla\varphi\ \text{and}\ \Delta\varphi=0\right\}.\end{cases}

(1.11)

It is well known, see for instance
[
16
]
, that the Magnetization operator
\mathbb{M}:\nabla\mathrm{H}_{\text{arm}}\rightarrow\nabla\mathrm{H}_{\text{arm}}
induces a complete orthonormal basis namely
\big(\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}},\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\big)_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}
. We are now able to present the first results of this research.

Theorem 1.1

Let a plasmonic nanoparticle occupying a domain
\Omega=\mathrm{z}+\delta B
which is of class
\mathcal{C}^{2}
. We choose the incident frequency of the form

\omega^{2}=\omega_{0}^{2}+\omega_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}\dfrac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}}{\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}(1-\varepsilon_{\infty}^{-1}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}})+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}})\mbox{ and }\gamma\omega\sim\delta^{h}.

(1.12)

Then we have the following approximation of the electric field with
|\mathrm{E}|^{2}=|\nabla\mathrm{H}|^{2}
, with
\mathrm{H}
as the solution to (
1.3
), as
\delta\to 0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}=\dfrac{1}{|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}|^{2}}\Bigg[|\mathrm{E}^{\textbf{in}}|^{2}(\mathrm{z})\Big(\int_{\mathrm{\Omega}}\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}\Big)^{2}+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{3}\Big)\Bigg]\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{h}<1,

(1.13)

where
\alpha=\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}(\omega)}-\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon_{m}}.
In addition, we have
|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}|\sim\delta^{h}
and
\Big(\int_{\mathrm{\Omega}}\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}\Big)^{2}\sim\delta^{2}
.

1.4.2
The electromagnetic field generated by dielectric nanoparticles

Let us recall that the volumetric Logarithmic potential operator
\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}
has a countable sequence of eigenvalues
\lambda_{\mathrm{n}}^{(\bm{\ell})}
and the corresponding eigen-functions
\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{n}}^{(\bm{\ell})}
that form an orthonormal basis in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. In the sequel, we will need the following properties:
\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{n}}^{(\bm{\ell})}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}\neq 0
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{n}}^{(\bm{\ell})}\sim\delta^{2}|\log\delta|.
These properties are shown to be true for the first eigenvalue/eigen-function
n=1
for
\Omega
being the disc of radius
\delta
, see
[
20
]
. We state the following theorem which was first derived in
[
20
]
but we extend it to the case of the Lorentz model for the permittivity.

Theorem 1.2

Let a dielectric nanoparticle occupying a domain
\Omega=\mathrm{z}+\delta B
which is of class
\mathcal{C}^{2}
.
2
2

2

Contrary to the plasmonic case, here we can reduce the regularity of
\Omega
to Lipschitz.
We choose the incident frequency of the form

\omega^{2}-\omega_{0}^{2}\sim-\delta^{2}|\log\delta|\Big(\overline{\lambda}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\omega_{0}^{2}\Big)\mbox{ and }\gamma\omega\sim\delta^{2}|\log\delta|^{1-\mathrm{h}-\mathrm{s}}\Big(\overline{\lambda}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\omega_{0}^{2}\Big)^{2}.

(1.14)

Then an approximate representation of the electric field with
\mathrm{E}
as a solution to (
1.5
) is as follows for
\delta\to 0

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}=\dfrac{1}{|1-\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}|^{2}}\Big[|\mathrm{E}^{\textbf{in}}|^{2}(\mathrm{z)}\Big(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}\Big)^{2}\;+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{2}|\log\delta|^{\mathrm{h}-1}\Big)\Big]\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{h}<1.

(1.15)

In addition, we have
|1-\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}|\sim(\log\delta)^{-h}
and
\Big(\int_{\mathrm{\Omega}}\mathrm{e}^{(\bm{\ell})}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}\Big)^{2}\sim\delta^{2}
.

1.4.3
The heat generated by the plasmonic/dielectric nanoparticles

The fundamental solution of the heat operator
\alpha\;\partial_{t}-\Delta
for the two dimensional spatial space is given by the expression

\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau):=\ \begin{cases}\frac{\alpha}{4\pi(t-\tau)}\textbf{exp}\Bigg(-\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{4(t-\tau)}\Bigg),\ \ \ t>\tau\\
0,\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(1.16)

The fundamental solutions for the interior and exterior heat equation (
1.8
) are
\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)
and
\Phi^{\mathrm{e}}(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)
respectively, which depend on the variables
\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}
and
\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}
.

We state the main result of our work.

Theorem 1.3

Let a Lorentzian nanoparticle, occupying a domain
\Omega=\mathrm{z}+\delta B
which is of class
\mathcal{C}^{2}
, be such that its heat coefficients
(\rho_{p},C_{c},\gamma_{c})
satisfy the conditions

\gamma_{p}=\overline{\gamma}_{p}\;\delta^{-2}~~\mbox{ and }~~\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{p}}\sim 1,~~\mbox{such that}~~\gamma_{m}<\sqrt{\overline{\gamma}_{p}\;\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{p}}},~~\delta\ll 1.

(1.17)

Let
\xi
such that
\textbf{dist}(\xi,\Omega)\sim\delta^{\mathrm{p}}

\Big(|\xi-z|\sim\delta^{\mathrm{p}}+\delta\Big)
.

1.

If we use incident frequency
\omega
satisfying (
1.12
), then for
\mathrm{r}<\frac{1}{2}
, if
\frac{1+2\mathrm{p}(1-\mathrm{r})}{2}<\mathrm{h}<1
, the heat conducted by the
plasmonic nanoparticle
, as a solution to (
1.8
), is given by, as
\delta\to 0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}(\xi,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}{\gamma_{m}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\Bigg[\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,t;z,\tau)d\tau\int_{\Omega}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}+\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi}\delta^{3-2\mathrm{p}(1-\mathrm{r})}\sqrt{\mathcal{K}^{(\mathrm{T_{0}})}_{\mathrm{r}}}\Bigg)\Bigg].

(1.18)

2.

If we use incident frequency
\omega
satisfying (
1.14
), then for
2\mathrm{p}(1-\mathrm{r})<1
, the heat conducted by the
dielectric nanoparticle
, as a solution to (
1.8
), is given by, as
\delta\to 0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}(\xi,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{m}}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\Bigg[\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,t;\mathrm{z},\tau)d\tau\int_{\Omega}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}+\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi}\delta^{5-2\mathrm{p}(1-\mathrm{r})}|\log\delta|^{\frac{3\mathrm{h}}{2}}\sqrt{\mathcal{K}^{(\mathrm{T_{0}})}_{\mathrm{r}}}\Bigg)\Bigg].

(1.19)

Here,
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{(\mathrm{T_{0}})}_{\mathrm{r}}:=\sup_{t\in(0,T_{0})}\int^{T_{0}}_{0}\frac{1}{(t-\tau)^{2r}}d\tau
and it makes sense if
\mathrm{r}<\frac{1}{2}
.

The above approximations can be further detailed by the following observations. Let
\mathrm{s}:=\frac{|\xi-\mathrm{z}|}{2\sqrt{t-\tau}}
. Then, as
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,t;z,\tau)d\tau
is independent of the space variable, and
t\leq\mathrm{T}_{0}
, it can be shown that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,t;z,\tau)d\tau=\frac{1}{2}\Large{\Gamma}\Big(0,\frac{|\xi-\mathrm{z}|^{2}}{4t}\Big).

(1.20)

Furthermore, for
|\xi-\mathrm{z}|<<t
, it is well known that

\displaystyle\Large{\Gamma}\Big(0,\frac{|\xi-\mathrm{z}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{t}}\Big)=\mathcal{E}_{1}\Big(\frac{|\xi-\mathrm{z}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{t}}\Big),

(1.21)

where
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is the exponential integral. Now, the exponential integral
\mathcal{E}_{1}
can be expanded as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}\Big(\frac{|\xi-\mathrm{z}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{t}}\Big)=-\gamma-\ln\frac{|\xi-\mathrm{z}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{t}}+\mathrm{e}_{1}\Big(\frac{|\xi-\mathrm{z}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{t}}\Big),

(1.22)

where
\gamma
is the Euler–Mascheroni constant and
\mathrm{e_{1}}
is a smooth function.

In addition, let us recall that
|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}|=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}})
. Therefore, we can derive a more precise dominating terms as follows

Corollary 1.3.1

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.3
and with the combination of Theorem
1.1
, we have the following approximation for a
plasmonic nanoparticle
occupying a domain
\Omega=\mathrm{z}+\delta B
, as
\delta\to 0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}(\xi,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{m}}}\;\Bigg[|\mathrm{E}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{z})|^{2}\Big(\int_{\Omega}{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}\Big)^{2}\;\log|\xi-\mathrm{z}|^{-1}\delta^{-2h}+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{3-2\mathrm{h}}\log|\xi-\mathrm{z}|^{-1}\Big)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{1-2\mathrm{p}(1-\mathrm{r})}\sqrt{\mathcal{K}^{(\mathrm{T_{0}})}_{\mathrm{r}}}\Big)\Bigg].

(1.23)

Similarly, let us recall that
|1-\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}\lambda^{(\bm{\ell})}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}|=\mathcal{O}(|\log\delta|^{-\mathrm{h}})
, where the electric permittivity
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}
derived from the Lorentz model for a dielectric nanoparticle. We state the following corollary.

Corollary 1.3.2

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.3
and with the combination of Theorem
1.2
, we have the following approximation for a
dielectric nanoparticle
occupying a domain
\Omega=\mathrm{z}+\delta B
, as
\delta\to 0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}(\xi,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{m}}}\Bigg[|\mathrm{E}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{z})|^{2}\Big(\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(\bm{\ell})}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}\Big)^{2}\log|\xi-\mathrm{z}|^{-1}|\log\delta|^{2\mathrm{h}}+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{2}|\log\delta|^{3\mathrm{h}-2}\log|\xi-\mathrm{z}|^{-1}\Big)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{3-2\mathrm{p}(1-\mathrm{r})}|\log\delta|^{\frac{3\mathrm{h}}{2}}\sqrt{\mathcal{K}^{(\mathrm{T_{0}})}_{\mathrm{r}}}\Big)\Bigg].

(1.24)

1.4.4
Discussion about the obtained results

We make the following two observations based on the two formulas (
1.3.1
) and (
1.3.2
).

1.

Using plasmonics. In this case, according to the choice of the incident frequency, from the Lorentz model, we have
\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})\sim\delta^{h}
. As
\ln{|\xi-z|}\sim p\;|\ln\delta|
, then from (
1.3.1
), recalling that
\displaystyle\Big(\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}\Big)^{2}\sim\delta^{2}
, we have

\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}(\xi,t)\sim p\;|\ln\delta|\delta^{2-h}(1+o(1)),\;\delta\ll 1.

(1.25)

We observe that the amount of heat can be enhanced till the order near to
\delta
.

2.

Using dielectrics. In this case, we have
\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})\sim\delta^{-2}(|\ln\delta|)^{-1-\mathrm{h}-\mathrm{s}}
. Therefore from (
1.3.2
), recalling that
\displaystyle\Big(\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(\bm{\ell})}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}\Big)^{2}\sim\delta^{2}
, we have

\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}(\xi,t)\sim p\;|\ln\delta|^{h-s}(1+o(1)),\;\delta\ll 1.

(1.26)

Choosing
\mathrm{s}=\mathrm{h}
, we have
\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}(\xi,t)\sim p
. Choosing
\mathrm{s}<\mathrm{h}
we get too much heat and for
s>h
, we get less heat.

Recall that
dist(\xi,\Omega)\sim\delta^{\mathrm{p}}
. In both the formulas (
1.25
) and (
1.26
), the parameter
p
indicates that the heat decreases when
dist(\xi,\Omega)
increases. For instance, the heat at the distance
dist(\xi,\Omega)=\delta^{\frac{1}{10}}
is of the order
\frac{1}{5}
the heat generated at a distance
dist(\xi,\Omega)=\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

In both cases, the multiplicative terms in the dominant parts are computable as the two respective quantities
\displaystyle\frac{\omega}{2\pi\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{m}}}\;|\mathrm{E}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{z})|^{2}\Big|\int_{\Omega}e^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}\Big|^{2}
and
\displaystyle\frac{\omega}{2\pi\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{m}}}|\mathrm{E}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{z})|^{2}\Big(\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(\bm{\ell})}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}\Big)^{2}
, for the plasmonics and the dielectrics, and they are at our disposal.

It is worth mentioning that according to the expansions above, dielectric nanoparticles provide more pronounce amount of heat around its surrounding than the plasmonic ones. Therefore, the choice of the incident frequency
\omega
makes a difference in generating the heat. Recall that, if it is chosen close to the undamped resonance frequency
\omega_{0}
, then the nanoparticle behaves as a dielectric one while if it is chosen via the electric plasma frequency
\omega_{p}
, then nanoparticle behaves a a plasmonic one.

The use of the heat generated by nanoparticles in different applications, as imaging, therapy etc, is well documented in the engineering literature, see
[
1
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
12
,
23
,
30
]
for instance. However, for our best knowledge, regarding its actual mathematical estimation, there is only the work
[
2
]
by Ammari et al. where the mathematical model was stated and the estimation where given on the surface of the nanoparticles.
To justify these expansions, in their work, they use semi-formal computations to characterize the dominant term. Precisely, they first use Fourier-Laplace transformation, then derive the small-volume approximations of in the Fourier-Laplace domain and then come back by inverse Fourier-Laplace transformation. However, in the Fourier-Laplace domain, the approximations of the fields are possible only at the ’low frequency’ range and we have no access to the approximation in the full frequency range. In the current work, we use time-domain techniques, instead, to provide the actual dominant part of the heat. In addition to its mathematical interests, it allows us to deal with more general sources of heat and not only locally supported (in time) ones (as the initial or instantaneous sources for instance).

The remaining part of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we state the equivalent boundary integral equations for the heat model after recalling the needed boundary integral operators and volume potentials. In Section
3
, we introduce the needed functional spaces i.e. the anisotropic Sobolev spaces, with their used properties. We also recall some basic mapping properties of the layer operators and volume potential for the heat equation on those spaces with their proper scales. The main steps of the proof of Theorem
1.3
are covered in Section
4
while those of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
are provided in Section
5
and Section
6
respectively. In order to simplify the exposition, some needed technical results and steps are moved to Appendix
7
.

2
Representation Formula, Integral Operators and Boundary Integral Equations for the Heat Equation

It is known that equations of boundary integral type are required to rephrase the transmission boundary value problem (
1.8
). With the use of both the interior and exterior Dirichlet problems between two media with different physical parameters, the boundary integral equation system can now be connected with the transmission condition. The following representation formula can therefore be used to determine the solution to the interior heat equation (
1.8
) using the direct approach, see
[
9
]
and
[
11
]
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}(\mathrm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t}\int_{\partial\Omega}\bigg(\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}(y,\tau)-\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}(y,\tau)\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{y}}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\bigg)d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}d\tau

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\int_{-\infty}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\Phi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t};\mathrm{y},\mathrm{\tau})\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}(\mathrm{y}){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{(0,T_{0})}d\mathrm{y}d\tau.

(2.1)

Before proceeding, we need to define the following boundary integral operators
\text{for}\ (\mathrm{x},t)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}
, namely the classical single-layer, double-layer and its spatial adjoint, and the hyper-singular heat operator as follows

\mathcal{S}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t):=\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}(y,\tau)d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}d\tau,

\mathcal{K}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t):=\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{y}}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}(y,\tau)d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}d\tau,

\mathcal{K^{*}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t):=\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}(y,\tau)d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}d\tau,

\mathbb{H}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}):=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{y}}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}(y,\tau)d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}d\tau

respectively. Furthermore, we refer to the Newtonian heat potential associated with the source term
f:=\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{(0,\mathrm{T_{0}})}
as

\mathcal{V}\Big[f\Big](\mathrm{x},t):=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)f(\mathrm{y},\tau)d\mathrm{y}d\tau.

As a result, from the interior problem, we derive the following boundary integral equations:

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)=\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathcal{V}\Big[f\Big](\mathrm{x},t),\ \text{for}\ \mathrm{x}\in\partial\Omega

(2.2)

and

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}^{*}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)=\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{V}\Big[f\Big](\mathrm{x},t),\ \text{for}\ \mathrm{x}\in\partial\Omega.

(2.3)

These equations together yield the Calderón system of boundary integral equations,
[
11
]

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\\
\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\end{pmatrix}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}&\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\\
\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}&\frac{1}{2}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}^{*}\end{pmatrix}}_{=:\ \mathcal{C}}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\\
\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathcal{V}[f]\\
\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{V}[f]\end{pmatrix}.

(2.4)

The operator
\mathcal{C}
is called the Calderón projection operator. A similar representation is also valid for the external heat problem in (
1.8
)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\\
\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\end{pmatrix}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}&-\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\\
-\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}&\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}^{*}\end{pmatrix}}_{=:\ \mathcal{C}}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\\
\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\end{pmatrix}

(2.5)

It is well known that
\mathcal{C}
is a projection, i.e.
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{2},
see
[
9
]
and
[
25
]
. Therefore, we have the following identity

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\mathbb{H}=(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}-\mathcal{K})(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\mathcal{K}).

(2.6)

The above identity will be useful to scale the hyper-singular heat operator and hence the Steklov-Poincare operator
\mathbb{A}^{\textbf{ext}}
. Now together with the transmission condition in (
1.8
) and the boundary integral equation described in (
2.4
), (
2.5
), we end up with the following system of boundary integral equation,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}^{*}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)=\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{V}\Big[f\Big](\mathrm{x},t)\\
\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}(\mathrm{x},t)=-\frac{\gamma_{\mathrm{m}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\mathbb{A}^{\textbf{ext}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)^{-1}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathcal{V}\Big[f\Big](\mathrm{x},t)\end{bmatrix},

(2.7)

associated with the source term
f:=\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{(0,\mathrm{T_{0}})}.

Further details on these derivations can be found in Appendix
7
.

3
Function Spaces, Properties and Scales of Integral Operators

We start this section with defining the anisotropic Sobolev spaces on space-time boundaries
\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}
on which the above system of integral equations (
2.7
) makes sense and can be inverted.

3.1
Function Spaces

Let
\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{s}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big),\Big[\mathrm{r},\mathrm{s}\in(0,1)\Big]
, denote the anisotropic Sobolev spaces with respect to the following norms, see
[
29
, Chapter 4]
and
[
31
]
,

\Big\|u\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{s}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}:=\Big\|u\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}+\Big|u\Big|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{r}}(\partial\Omega)\Big)}^{2}+\Big|u\Big|_{\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{s}}\Big(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)\Big)}^{2},

(3.1)

where

\Big|u\Big|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{r}}(\partial\Omega)\Big)}^{2}:=\int_{\partial\Omega}\int_{\partial\Omega}\dfrac{\|u(\mathrm{x},\cdot)-u(\mathrm{y},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{n-1+2\mathrm{r}}}d\sigma_{\mathrm{x}}d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}},

(3.2)

and

\Big|u\Big|_{\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{s}}\Big(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)\Big)}^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{\|u(\cdot,t)-u(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}}{|t-\tau|^{1+2\mathrm{s}}}dtd\tau.

(3.3)

Then, we define the negative-ordered anisotropic Sobolev space as the dual space of
\mathrm{H}^{-\mathrm{r},-\mathrm{s}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)
when
\mathrm{r},\mathrm{s}<0
. We denote it by
\Big[\mathrm{H}^{-\mathrm{r},-\mathrm{s}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\Big]^{\prime}
and equip it with the norm

\displaystyle\Big\|u\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{s}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}=\sup_{0\neq\varphi\in\mathrm{H}^{-\mathrm{r},-\mathrm{s}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\dfrac{\Big|\Big\langle u,\varphi\Big\rangle_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}\Big|}{\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\mathrm{r},-\mathrm{s}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}}.

(3.4)

Furthermore, we introduce the following anisotropic Sobolev space
\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)
with respect to the following norm

\displaystyle\Big\|u\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\Big[|\nabla_{\textbf{tan}}u(\mathrm{x},t)|^{2}+u^{2}(\mathrm{x},t)+\Big(\partial_{t}^{\frac{1}{2}}u(\mathrm{x},t)\Big)^{2}\Big]d\sigma_{\mathrm{x}}d\mathrm{t},

(3.5)

where the tangential gradient
\nabla_{\textbf{tan}}u
on
\partial\Omega
is described as
\nabla_{\textbf{tan}}u:=\nabla u-(\partial_{\nu}u)\nu
and we denote by
\partial_{t}^{\frac{1}{2}}
the fractional time derivative of order
\frac{1}{2}
. Next, due to the interpolation theory, we can state the following result regarding the anisotropic space
\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}(\Omega)
, see
[
29
, Chapter 2]
for detailed description. We have for
\mathrm{r},\mathrm{s}\geq 0
and
\theta\in(0,1)

\displaystyle\Bigg[\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big),\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big((\partial\Omega)_{T}\Big)\Bigg]_{\theta=\frac{1}{2}}=\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big((\partial\Omega)_{T}\Big).

(3.6)

In addition, we point out the following interpolation inequality

\displaystyle\Big\|u\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big((\partial\Omega)_{T}\Big)}\lesssim\Big\|u\Big\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big((\partial\Omega)_{T}\Big)}\Big\|u\Big\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big((\partial\Omega)_{T}\Big)}.

(3.7)

In the following paragraphs, we review the mapping properties of the boundary integral operators mentioned above. It has been well established that all boundary integral operators exhibit a mapping property, see, for instance
[
4
]
,
[
9
]
, and
[
15
]
. In our analysis, we need the
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-regularity of the boundary
\partial\Omega
to study the magnetization operator, otherwise only the Lipschitz regularity is enough.

3.2
Mapping Properties of the Integral Operators

Lemma 3.1

[
31
, Section 5]

On smooth
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-boundary
\partial\Omega
and for all
\mathrm{r}\geq 0
the single layer heat operator
\mathcal{S}
maps
H^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{r},(-\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{r})/2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)
to
H^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{r},(\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{r})/2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)
isomorphically.

This lemma still holds for a Lipschitz boundary, if
\mathrm{r}
is in a certain finite range. Next, we state the following result.

Lemma 3.2

[
24
]

Let
\Omega
be the domain above the graph of a Lipschitz function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{n}-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\ (\mathrm{n}\geq 2).
Then the operator

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I+\mathcal{K}^{*}:L^{\mathrm{2}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\rightarrow L^{\mathrm{2}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)

(3.8)

is invertible.

Moreover, the operators

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I+\mathcal{K}:\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\rightarrow\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big),

(3.9)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I+\mathcal{K}:H^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\rightarrow H^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)

(3.10)

are invertible.

Consequently, with the help of interpolation theory, we can demonstrate the following corollary to the above lemma:

Corollary 3.2.1

The operators

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I+\mathcal{K}:\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big),

(3.11)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I+\mathcal{K}^{*}:\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)

(3.12)

are invertible.

Then, we state the mapping property of Newtonian heat potential, see for instance
[
9
]
.

Lemma 3.3

The convolution between the fundamental solution
\Phi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t};\mathrm{y},\mathrm{\tau})
and density i.e. the Newtonian heat potential

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}:H^{r,\frac{r}{2}}_{\text{comp}}\Big(\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{n}}\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\rightarrow H^{r+2,\frac{r}{2}+1}_{\text{loc}}\Big(\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{n}}\times\mathbb{R}\Big)

(3.13)

is linear and bounded for any
\mathrm{r}\in\mathbb{R}.

Here, we denote by
H^{\mathrm{r},\frac{\mathrm{r}}{2}}_{\text{comp}}\Big(\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{n}}\times(0,\mathrm{T})\Big)
set of functions which has compact support in space variable and by
H^{\mathrm{r}+2,\frac{\mathrm{r}}{2}+1}_{\text{loc}}\Big(\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{n}}\times(0,\mathrm{T})\Big)
, we describe the local behaviour in the space variable. Now, it is clear that for
\mathrm{r}=-1
and by restriction of the domain, we find the Newtonian heat potential

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}:\Big[H^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\Big]^{\prime}\rightarrow\Big[H^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\Big]

(3.14)

is linear and bounded.

The next step is to state the trace theorem. See, for example,
[
29
, Theorem 2.1]
,
[
31
, Chapter 2]
.

Lemma 3.4

(Trace Theorem) The interior trace operator

\displaystyle\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}:H^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)

(3.15)

is linear and bounded.

Now, regarding the Newtonian heat potential, we restrict the domain to
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)
as we consider the corresponding source term belonging to that space. Consequently, from Lemma
3.3
and trace theorem we deduce the following corollary

Corollary 3.4.1

The following operator

\displaystyle\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathcal{V}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)

(3.16)

is linear and continuous.

As we proceed, we introduce an anisotropic Sobolev space that is required to describe the mapping properties of the interior and exterior Neumann trace operators, as well as their application to the Newtonian heat operator.

\displaystyle H^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R},\mathcal{L}\Big):=\Big\{\mathrm{u}\in H^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big):\mathcal{L}u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\Big\},

(3.17)

where,
\mathcal{L}:=\alpha\partial_{t}-\Delta
represents the corresponding heat differential operator. Now we will state the following lemma, see
[
9
, Proposition 2.18]
, for details.

Lemma 3.5

The interior Neumann Trace Operator

\displaystyle\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}:H^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R},\mathcal{L}\Big)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)

(3.18)

defines a linear bounded operator.

Furthermore, from Lemma
3.3
, after restricting the domain to
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)
and considering that the Newtonian heat potential satisfies the corresponding heat equation, we obtain the following corollary

Corollary 3.5.1

The following operator

\displaystyle\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathcal{V}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)

(3.19)

is linear and continuous.

Also, let us define the initial heat potential for
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)
as follows

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}[f](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})=\int_{\Omega}\Phi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t};\mathrm{y})f(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y},

(3.20)

which enjoys the following mapping property. For more details we refer to
[
11
, Theorem 4.4]
and
[
31
, Section 7]
.

Lemma 3.6

The initial heat operator
\mathbb{I}:\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\mathrm{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\Big)
is linear and bounded.

3.3
Scales for the Function Spaces

Using the same notation as in
[
34
]
, we consider a nanoparticle occupying a domain
\Omega=\delta\mathrm{B}+\mathrm{z}
, where
\mathrm{B}
is centered at the origin and
|\mathrm{B}|\sim 1.
Moreover, we use the notation below in defining functions
\varphi
and
\psi
on
\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}
and
\partial\mathrm{B}\times\mathbb{R}
, respectively,

\hat{\varphi}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})=\varphi^{\Lambda}(\eta,\tilde{\tau}):=\varphi(\delta\eta+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau}),\quad\quad\quad\check{\psi}(\mathrm{x},t)=\psi^{\vee}(\mathrm{x},t):=\psi\Bigg(\frac{\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{z}}{\delta},\frac{t}{\alpha\delta^{2}}\Bigg)

(3.21)

for
(\mathrm{x},t)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}
and
(\eta,\tilde{\tau})\in\partial B\times\mathbb{R}
respectively. Our next step is to derive the following lemma.

Lemma 3.7

Suppose
0<\delta\leq 1
,
\Omega:=\delta B+\mathrm{z}
and
t:=\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{t}
. Then for
\varphi\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)
and
\psi\in\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)
, we have the following scales

\displaystyle\alpha\delta^{3}\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}\leq\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}\leq\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{2}\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}

(3.22)

and

\displaystyle\alpha^{\frac{3}{4}}\delta^{2}\Big\|\hat{\psi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\leq\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\leq\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{\frac{3}{2}}\Big\|\hat{\psi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}.

(3.23)

Proof.

Let us set
\mathrm{x}:=\delta\xi+\mathrm{z}
,
\mathrm{y}:=\delta\eta+\mathrm{z}
,
t:=\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{t}
and
\tau=\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau}
. As a first step, we need to scale
\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{\partial\Omega}|\varphi|^{2}(\mathrm{x},t)d\sigma_{\mathrm{x}}dt=\alpha\delta^{3}\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{\partial B}|\varphi|^{2}(\delta\eta+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau})d\sigma_{\eta}d\tilde{\tau}=\alpha\delta^{3}\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}

(3.24)

Now, in order to scale the norms (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), we need to estimate
\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}
and
\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}
respectively.
We have

\displaystyle\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}=\int_{-\infty}^{T}|\varphi|^{2}(\mathrm{x},t)dt=\alpha\delta^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}|\varphi|^{2}(\delta\eta+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau})d\tilde{\tau}=\alpha\delta^{2}\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}

(3.25)

and

\displaystyle\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}=\int_{\partial\Omega}|\varphi|^{2}(\mathrm{x},t)d\sigma(\mathrm{x})=\delta\int_{\partial B}|\varphi|^{2}(\delta\eta+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau})d\sigma_{\eta}=\delta\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial B)}^{2}.

(3.26)

Therefore, using (
3.25
) and (
3.26
), we estimate the following norms

\displaystyle\Big|\varphi\Big|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathbb{R};H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega)\Big)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega}\int_{\partial\Omega}\dfrac{\|\varphi(x,\cdot)-\varphi(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{|x-y|^{2}}d\sigma_{\mathrm{x}}d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}

\displaystyle=\delta^{2}\int_{\partial B}\int_{\partial B}\dfrac{\alpha\delta^{2}\|\hat{\varphi}(\xi,\cdot)-\hat{\varphi}(\eta,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{\delta^{2}|\xi-\eta|^{2}}d\sigma_{\xi}d\sigma_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{2}\Big|\hat{\varphi}\Big|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathbb{R};H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial B)\Big)}^{2}.

(3.27)

Again, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\varphi\Big|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)\Big)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{-\infty}^{T}\dfrac{\|\varphi(\cdot,t)-\varphi(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}}{|t-\tau|^{\frac{3}{2}}}dtd\tau

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}\delta^{4}\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\dfrac{\delta\|\varphi(\cdot,\tilde{t})-\varphi(\cdot,\tilde{\tau})\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial B)}^{2}}{\alpha^{\frac{3}{2}}\delta^{3}|\tilde{t}-\tilde{\tau}|^{\frac{3}{2}}}d\tilde{t}d\tilde{\tau}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{2}\Big|\hat{\varphi}\Big|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial B)\Big)}^{2}

(3.28)

Finally, considering the norm (
3.3
), (
3.3
), and (
3.3
), noticing the fact
\alpha\delta^{3}\leq\alpha\delta^{2}\leq\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{2}
, we prove the first double inequality,

\displaystyle\alpha\delta^{3}\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}\leq\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}\leq\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{2}\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}.

(3.29)

In order to derive the second inequality, we need to scale
\Big\langle u,\psi\Big\rangle_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}
.

\displaystyle\Big\langle u,\psi\Big\rangle_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{\partial\Omega}u(y,\tau)\psi(y,\tau)d\mathrm{y}d\tau

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{3}\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{\partial B}u(\delta\eta+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau})\psi(\delta\eta+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau})d\sigma_{\eta}d\tilde{\tau}

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{3}\Big\langle\hat{u},\hat{\psi}\Big\rangle_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}

(3.30)

Hence, from the definition of norm (
3.40
), we get our desired second inequality

\displaystyle\alpha^{\frac{3}{4}}\delta^{2}\Big\|\hat{\psi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\leq\Big\|\psi\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\leq\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{\frac{3}{2}}\Big\|\hat{\psi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}.

(3.31)

Hence it completes the proof.

Lemma 3.8

For
\partial_{t}\varphi\in\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)
we have the following scales

\displaystyle\alpha^{-\frac{1}{4}}\Big\|\partial_{\tilde{t}}\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\leq\Big\|\partial_{t}\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\leq\alpha^{-\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|\partial_{\tilde{t}}\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}.

(3.32)

Proof.

As a first step, we will do the scaling of
\Big\|\partial_{t}\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\Big\|\partial_{t}\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{\partial\Omega}|\partial_{t}\varphi(\mathrm{x},t)|^{2}d\sigma_{\mathrm{x}}dt=\alpha\delta^{3}\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{\partial B}\alpha^{-2}\delta^{-4}|\partial_{\tilde{t}}\varphi(\delta\eta+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau})|^{2}d\sigma_{\eta}d\tilde{\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha\delta}\Big\|\partial_{\tilde{t}}\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}.

(3.33)

Next, we calculate the following product.

\displaystyle\Big\langle u,\partial_{t}\varphi\Big\rangle_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\partial\Omega}u(\mathrm{y},\tau)\partial_{t}\varphi(\mathrm{x},t)d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}d\tau

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{3}\int_{0}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{\partial B}\alpha^{-1}\delta^{-2}\partial_{\tilde{t}}\varphi(\delta\eta+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau})u(\delta\eta+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau})d\sigma_{\eta}d\tilde{\tau}

\displaystyle=\delta\int_{0}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{\partial B}\partial_{\tilde{t}}\hat{\varphi}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})\hat{u}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})d\sigma_{\eta}d\tilde{\tau}

\displaystyle=\delta\Big\langle\hat{u},\partial_{\tilde{t}}\hat{\varphi}\Big\rangle_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}

(3.34)

Then by Lemma
3.7
and from the previous estimate we obtain the desired inequality

\displaystyle\alpha^{-\frac{1}{4}}\Big\|\partial_{\tilde{t}}\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\leq\Big\|\partial_{t}\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\leq\alpha^{-\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|\partial_{\tilde{t}}\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}.

(3.35)

Lemma 3.9

For
\varphi\in H^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\Big)
and
\psi\in H^{-1,-\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\Big)
we have the following scales

\displaystyle\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{2}\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(B\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\Big)}\leq\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\Big)}\leq\delta\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(B\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\Big)}

(3.36)

and

\displaystyle\alpha\delta^{3}\Big\|\hat{\psi}\Big\|_{H^{-1,-\frac{1}{2}}\Big(B\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\Big)}\leq\Big\|\psi\Big\|_{H^{-1,-\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\Big)}\leq\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{2}\Big\|\hat{\psi}\Big\|_{H^{-1,-\frac{1}{2}}\Big(B\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\Big)}.

(3.37)

Proof.
For
\varphi\in H^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R_{+}}\Big)
we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\psi\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R_{+}}\Big)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\Big[|\nabla_{\textbf{tan}}\varphi(\mathrm{x},t)|^{2}+|\varphi(\mathrm{x},t)|^{2}+\Big(\partial_{t}^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi(\mathrm{x},t)\Big)^{2}\Big]d\mathrm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{4}\int_{0}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{B}\Big[\delta^{-2}|\nabla_{\textbf{tan}}\varphi(\delta\xi+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{t})|^{2}+|\varphi(\delta\xi+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{t})|^{2}\Big]d\xi d\tilde{t}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{B}\alpha^{-1}\delta^{-2}\Big(\partial_{t}^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi(\delta\xi+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{t})\Big)^{2}d\xi d\tilde{t}

(3.38)

Now, observing the fact that
\alpha\delta^{4}\leq\delta^{2}
, we obtain the desired first inequality

\displaystyle\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{2}\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(B\times\mathbb{R_{+}}\Big)}\leq\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R_{+}}\Big)}\leq\delta\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(B\times\mathbb{R_{+}}\Big)}.

(3.39)

Next, from the definition of norm for the dual space we know that

\displaystyle\Big\|\psi\Big\|_{H^{-1,-\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R_{+}}\Big)}=\sup_{0\neq u\in H^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R_{+}}\Big)}\dfrac{\Big|\Big\langle\psi,u\Big\rangle_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R_{+}}}\Big|}{\Big\|u\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1,\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R_{+}}\Big)}}.

(3.40)

Now, to get the desired inequality we need to scale the scalar product
\Big\langle\psi,u\Big\rangle_{(\partial\Omega)_{T}}
.

\displaystyle\Big\langle\psi,u\Big\rangle_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R_{+}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}u(\mathrm{y},\tau)\psi(\mathrm{y},\tau)d\mathrm{y}d\tau

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{4}\int_{0}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{B}\hat{u}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})\hat{\psi}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})d\eta d\tilde{\tau}

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{4}\Big\langle\hat{\psi},\hat{u}\Big\rangle_{B\times\mathbb{R_{+}}}

(3.41)

Consequently, this leads to

\displaystyle\alpha\delta^{3}\Big\|\hat{\psi}\Big\|_{H^{-1,-\frac{1}{2}}\Big(B\times\mathbb{R_{+}}\Big)}\leq\Big\|\psi\Big\|_{H^{-1,-\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R_{+}}\Big)}\leq\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{2}\Big\|\hat{\psi}\Big\|_{H^{-1,-\frac{1}{2}}\Big(B\times\mathbb{R_{+}}\Big)}.

(3.42)

The next step is to state and derive the following lemmas, which will be useful in estimating the hyper-singular and Steklov-Poincare operators, as well as providing an exact dominating term for the estimated heat potential.

3.4
Scales for the Integral Operators

Lemma 3.10

For
\varphi\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)
and
\psi\in\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)
, we have the following estimate

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\psi\Big](\mathrm{x},t)=\delta\Big(\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big)^{\vee}\ \text{and}\ \mathcal{S}^{-1}_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\varphi\Big](\mathrm{x},t)=\delta^{-1}\Big(\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\hat{\varphi}\Big]\Big)^{\vee}.

(3.43)

Based on the above estimates, we obtain the following one

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{S}^{-1}_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}\Big\|_{\mathcal{L}{\Big(\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big),\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\Big)}\leq\delta^{-1}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big\|_{\mathcal{L}{\Big(\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big),\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big)\Big)},

(3.44)

where,
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
represents the single layer operator corresponding to the fundamental solution with
\alpha:=1
.

Proof.
As usual, we set
\mathrm{x}:=\delta\xi+\mathrm{z}
,
\mathrm{y}:=\delta\eta+\mathrm{z}
,
t:=\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{t}
and
\tau:=\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau}
. We write

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\psi\Big](\mathrm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\psi(\mathrm{y},\tau)d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\tilde{t}}\int_{\partial B}\dfrac{\alpha}{4\pi\alpha\delta^{2}(\tilde{t}-\tilde{\tau})}e^{-\dfrac{\alpha\delta^{2}|\xi-\eta|^{2}}{4\alpha\delta^{2}(\tilde{t}-\tilde{\tau})}}\psi(\delta\eta+\mathrm{z},\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau})\delta^{3}d\sigma_{\eta}d\tilde{\tau}

\displaystyle=\delta\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\tilde{t}}\int_{\partial B}\dfrac{1}{4\pi(\tilde{t}-\tilde{\tau})}e^{-\dfrac{|\xi-\eta|^{2}}{4(\tilde{t}-\tilde{\tau})}}\hat{\psi}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})d\sigma_{\eta}d\hat{\tau}

\displaystyle=\delta\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big](\xi,\tilde{t})

(3.45)

which gives the first identity. The second identity can be derived from the following

\mathcal{S}_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\Big(\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\hat{\varphi}\Big]\Big)^{\vee}\Big]=\delta\Bigg(\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big(\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\hat{\varphi}\Big]\Big)\Bigg)^{\vee}=\delta(\hat{\varphi})^{\vee}=\delta\varphi.

Using the definition of the operator norm, to derive the estimate (
3.44
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{S}^{-1}_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}\Big\|_{\mathcal{L}{\Big(\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big),\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\Big)}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{0\neq\varphi\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\dfrac{\Big\|\mathcal{S}^{-1}_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}[\varphi]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}}{\Big\|\varphi\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0\neq\varphi\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\dfrac{\alpha\delta^{\frac{3}{2}}\Big\|\Big(\mathcal{S}^{-1}_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}[\varphi]\Big)^{\wedge}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}}{\alpha\delta^{\frac{3}{2}}\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{0\neq\hat{\varphi}\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\dfrac{\Big\|\Big(\delta^{-1}\Big(\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\hat{\varphi}\Big]\Big)^{\vee}\Big)^{\wedge}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}}{\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{0\neq\hat{\varphi}\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\dfrac{\delta^{-1}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\hat{\varphi}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}}{\Big\|\hat{\varphi}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}}

\displaystyle=\delta^{-1}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big\|_{\mathcal{L}{\Big(\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big),\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}\Big)}.

(3.46)

The proof is thus complete.

Lemma 3.11

Suppose
0<\delta\leq 1
and
\Omega=\delta B+\mathrm{z}
and
t=\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{t}
. Then for
\psi\in\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)
we have

\displaystyle\alpha\delta^{5}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}\leq\Big\|\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}\leq\alpha^{\frac{1}{{}^{2}}}\delta^{4}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}.

(3.47)

Proof.
Based on the proof of Lemma
3.10
, we conclude that

\mathcal{S}_{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\psi\Big](\mathrm{x},t)=\delta\Big(\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\partial B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big)^{\vee},

where,
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
is the single layer operator independent of the diffusion constant
\alpha
is set to be
1
. In order to scale
\Big\|\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}
, from the definition of norm (
3.1
), we need first to consider
\Big\|\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}.

Indeed,

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{S}\Big[\Psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{\partial\Omega}\Bigg|\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{\partial\Omega}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\psi(y,\tau)d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}d\tau\Bigg|^{2}d\sigma_{\mathrm{x}}dt

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{5}\int_{-\infty}^{\mathrm{T}_{\delta}}\int_{\partial B}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\mathrm{T}_{\delta}}\int_{\partial B}\Phi(\xi,\tilde{t};\eta,\tilde{\tau})\hat{\psi}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})d\sigma_{\eta}d\tilde{\tau}\Bigg|^{2}d\sigma_{\xi}d\tilde{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{5}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}.

(3.48)

Now, in order to estimate (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), we need to scale
\Big\|\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}
and
\Big\|\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}
respectively.

So,

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{T}\Bigg|\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{\partial\Omega}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\psi(y,\tau)d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}d\tau\Bigg|^{2}dt

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{4}\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{\partial B}\Phi(\xi,\tilde{t};\eta,\tilde{\tau})\hat{\psi}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})d\sigma_{\eta}d\tilde{\tau}\Bigg|^{2}d\tilde{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{4}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

(3.49)

Also,

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega}\Bigg|\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{\partial\Omega}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\psi(y,\tau)d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}d\tau\Bigg|^{2}d\sigma_{\mathrm{x}}

\displaystyle=\delta^{3}\int_{\partial B}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{\partial B}\Phi(\xi,\tilde{t};\eta,\tilde{\tau})\hat{\psi}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})d\sigma_{\eta}d\tilde{\tau}\Bigg|^{2}d\sigma_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\delta^{3}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial B)}^{2}.

(3.50)

So, using (
3.4
) and (
3.4
), we calculate the following norms

\displaystyle\Big|\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathbb{R};H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega)\Big)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega}\int_{\partial\Omega}\dfrac{\Big\|\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big](x,\cdot)-\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big](y,\cdot)\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{|x-y|^{2}}d\sigma_{\mathrm{x}}d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}

\displaystyle=\delta^{2}\int_{\partial B}\int_{\partial B}\dfrac{\alpha\delta^{4}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big](\xi,\cdot)-\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big](\eta,\cdot)\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{\delta^{2}|\xi-\eta|^{2}}d\sigma_{\xi}d\sigma_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{4}\Big|\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathbb{R};H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial B)\Big)}^{2}

(3.51)

Again,

\displaystyle\Big|\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)\Big)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{-\infty}^{T}\dfrac{\Big\|\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big](\cdot,t)-\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big](\cdot,\tau)\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}}{|t-\tau|^{\frac{3}{2}}}dtd\tau

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}\delta^{4}\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\dfrac{\delta^{3}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big](\xi,\cdot)-\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big](\eta,\cdot)\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial B)}^{2}}{\alpha^{\frac{3}{2}}\delta^{3}|\tilde{t}-\tilde{\tau}|^{\frac{3}{2}}}d\tilde{t}d\tilde{\tau}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{4}\Big|\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial B)\Big)}^{2}

(3.52)

In this case, we obtain our desired result by estimating the norms (
3.4
), (
3.4
) and (
3.4
), the norm (
3.1
) and, noticing the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\alpha\delta^{5}}\leq\alpha\delta^{4}\leq\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{4}}
,

\displaystyle\alpha\delta^{5}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}\leq\Big\|\mathcal{S}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}\leq\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{4}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{S}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}.

(3.53)

Lemma 3.12

For
\psi\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)
we have the following identities

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\psi\Big](\mathrm{x},t)=\delta^{2}\Big(\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big)^{\vee}

(3.54)

and

\displaystyle\alpha\delta^{7}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{V}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}\leq\Big\|\mathcal{V}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}\leq\alpha^{\frac{1}{{}^{2}}}\delta^{6}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{V}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}.

(3.55)

Proof.
Setting again
\mathrm{x}:=\delta\xi+\mathrm{z}
,
\mathrm{y}:=\delta\eta+\mathrm{z}
,
t:=\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{t}
and
\tau:=\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\psi\Big](\mathrm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\psi(y,\tau)d\mathrm{y}d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{B}\dfrac{\alpha}{4\pi\alpha\delta^{2}(\tilde{t}-\tilde{\tau})}e^{-\dfrac{\alpha\delta^{2}|\xi-\eta|^{2}}{4\alpha\delta^{2}(\tilde{t}-\tilde{\tau})}}\hat{\psi}(\delta\eta+\mathrm{z},\alpha\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau})\alpha\delta^{4}d\eta d\tilde{\tau}

\displaystyle=\delta^{2}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{B}\dfrac{1}{4\pi(\tilde{t}-\tilde{\tau})}e^{-\dfrac{|\xi-\eta|^{2}}{4(\tilde{t}-\tilde{\tau})}}\hat{\psi}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})d\eta d\tilde{\tau}

\displaystyle=\delta^{2}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{B\times\mathbb{R}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big](\xi,\tilde{t}).

(3.56)

where,
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
is the Newtonian heat operator corresponding to
\alpha=1
and to the source
\psi.

In order to scale
\Big\|\mathcal{V}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}
, from the definition of norm (
3.1
), we need to first calculate
\Big\|\mathcal{V}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}.

Indeed,

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{V}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{\partial\Omega}\Bigg|\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\Psi(y,\tau)d\mathrm{y}d\tau\Bigg|^{2}d\sigma_{\mathrm{x}}dt

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{7}\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{\partial B}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{B}\Phi(\xi,\tilde{t};\eta,\tilde{\tau})\hat{\psi}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})d\eta d\tilde{\tau}\Bigg|^{2}d\sigma_{\xi}d\tilde{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{7}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{V}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\partial B\times\mathbb{R}\Big)}^{2}.

(3.57)

Further, in order to calculate (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), we need to scale the following
\Big\|\mathcal{V}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
and
\Big\|\mathcal{V}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}
respectively. Indeed,

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{V}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{T}\Bigg|\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\Psi(y,\tau)d\mathrm{y}d\tau\Bigg|^{2}dt

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{6}\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\delta}}\int_{B}\Phi(\xi,\tilde{t};\eta,\tilde{\tau})\hat{\psi}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})d\eta d\tilde{\tau}\Bigg|^{2}d\hat{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha\delta^{6}\Big\|\overline{\mathcal{V}}\Big[\hat{\psi}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}

(3.58)

and

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{V}\Big[\psi\Big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega}\Bigg|\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\Psi(y,\tau)d\mathrm{y}d\tau\Bigg|^{2}d\sigma_{\mathrm{x}}
