2\mathrm{p}(1-\mathrm{r})<1
we deduce

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}(\xi,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{m}}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\Bigg[\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,t;\mathrm{z},\tau)|\mathrm{E}|^{2}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}d\tau+\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi}\delta^{5-2\mathrm{p}(1-\mathrm{r})}|\log\delta|^{\frac{3\mathrm{h}}{2}}\sqrt{\mathcal{K}^{(\mathrm{T_{0}})}_{\mathrm{r}}}\Bigg)\Bigg].

(4.71)

This completes the proof of the second part of the Theorem
1.3
.

5
Proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section, we show the asymptotic analysis of the solution to (
1.3
) as
\delta\to 0
when a plasmonic nanoparticle occupy a bounded domain
\Omega=\mathrm{z}+\delta B.
We begin to transform the equation (
1.3
) into an integral equation. For this, let us recall the fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation in two dimensions

\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})=\dfrac{i}{4}\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{0}(\mathrm{k}|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|)\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{x}\neq\mathrm{y},

where
\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{0}
is the Hankel function of the first kind of order zero.
The unique solution of the equation (
1.3
) leads to the Lippmann–Schwinger equation

\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{x})-\alpha\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}=\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{x}),

(5.1)

where
\alpha=\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon_{c}(\omega)}-\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon_{m}}.
We then take gradient of the equation (
5.1
) to get

{}\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{x})-\alpha\Big(\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}\Big)=\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{x})

(5.2)

Then, we rewrite the above equation as

\displaystyle\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{x})-\alpha\Big(\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}\Big)=\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{x})+\alpha\Big(\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\Big(\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}-\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\Big)(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}\Big),

(5.3)

where
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})
is the fundamental solution of the Laplacian.

We study the system of integral equations (
5.3
) in the Hilbert space of vector-valued function
\Big(\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)\Big)^{2}.
For the sake of simplicity, we use
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)=\Big(\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)\Big)^{2}
. This space can be decomposed into three sub-spaces as a direct sum as following, see
[
33
]
,

\mathbb{L}^{2}=\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{div},0)\oplus\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{curl},0)\oplus\nabla H_{arm},

where we define these three sub-spaces as follows

{}\begin{cases}\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{div},0)=\left\{u\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega):\nabla\cdot u=0\ \text{and}\ u\cdot\nu=0\right\},\\
\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{curl},0)=\left\{u\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega):\nabla\times u=0\ \text{and}\ u\times\nu=0\right\},\\
\nabla H_{arm}=\left\{u\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega):\exists\ \varphi\ \text{s.t.}\ u=\nabla\varphi\ \text{and}\ \Delta\varphi=0\right\}.\end{cases}

(5.4)

Consider
\big(\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\big)_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\big(\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\big)_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}
to be any orthonormal basis of the sub-spaces
\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{div},0)
and
\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{curl},0)
respectively. But for the sub-space
\nabla H_{arm}
, we consider the complete orthonormal basis
\big(\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}},\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
derived as the eigenfunctions of the magnetization operator
\mathbb{M}:\nabla H_{arm}\rightarrow\nabla H_{arm},
defined by

\mathbb{M}\Big[\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big](\mathrm{x})=\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}.

(5.5)

In addition, we have the expansion

\partial_{\mathrm{ij}}\Big(\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}-\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\Big)(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})=2a\delta_{\mathrm{ij}}\log|x-y|+2\mathrm{a}\dfrac{(\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y})_{\mathrm{i}}(\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y})_{\mathrm{j}}}{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}+2\mathrm{b}\delta_{\mathrm{ij}}+\mathcal{O}(|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|),\quad\mathrm{x}\sim\mathrm{y}.

(5.6)

from which we deduce smoothing properties of the operator
\mathbb{T}
defined by

\mathbb{T}\Big[\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big](\mathrm{x})=\int_{\Omega}\nabla\nabla\cdot\Big(\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}-\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\Big)(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}.

(5.7)

In particular, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 5.1

The operator
\mathbb{T}
is a bounded operator from
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{2}(\Omega).

We will then take projection of the equation (
5.3
) into each of the above described sub-spaces.

5.1
Projection on
\textbf{H}_{0}(\text{div},0)

We begin by considering the orthonormal basis
\big(\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\big)_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}
and take the projection of the equation (
5.3
) into the subspace
\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{div},0)
to get

\displaystyle\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H};\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}-\alpha\Big\langle\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y};\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\alpha\Big\langle\mathbb{T}\Big[\nabla\mathrm{H}\big];\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

(5.8)

Next, we evaluate the second term of the left hand of the equation (
5.8
) with the usual induced inner product of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
. Then, from the definition of the sub-space
\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{div},0)
and integration by parts, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\langle\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y};\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\cdot\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}\nabla\cdot\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\ \int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}

\displaystyle+\int_{\partial\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\cdot\nu\ \int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}=0.

(5.9)

Using the same arguments, we have

\displaystyle\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}=0.

(5.10)

Hence combining equations (
5.1
), (
5.10
) and plugging these into the equation (
5.8
) we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H};\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}=\Big\langle\mathbb{T}\Big[\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big];\ \mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

(5.11)

5.2
Projection on
\textbf{H}_{0}(\text{curl},0)

Considering the set of orthonormal basis
\big(\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\big)_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}
and taking the projection of the equation (
5.3
) into the subspace
\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{curl},0)
we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}-\alpha\Big\langle\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\alpha\Big\langle\mathbb{T}\Big[\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big];\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

(5.12)

Next, we evaluate the second term of the left hand of the equation (
5.12
) with the usual induced inner product of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
. As before, we consider the second sub-space
\textbf{H}_{0}(\textbf{curl},0)
. We use the vector identity
\nabla\nabla\cdot\textbf{f}=\nabla\times\nabla\times\textbf{f}+\Delta\textbf{f}
and the vector integration formula to deduce that

\displaystyle\Big\langle\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}\ ;\ \mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\textbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\cdot\ \nabla\times\Big(\nabla\times\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}\Big)

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\ \Delta\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\nabla\times\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\cdot\nabla\times\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}

\displaystyle+\int_{\partial\Omega}\nu\times\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\cdot\nabla\times\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Delta\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\ \Delta\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}=\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

(5.13)

Therefore, from the equation (
5.2
) and plugging it in (
5.12
) we get

\displaystyle(1-\alpha)\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\ =\ \Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}+\Big\langle\mathbb{T}\Big[\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big];\ \mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

(5.14)

5.3
Projection on
\nabla\mathrm{H}_{\textbf{arm}}

We know that the magnetization operator
\mathbb{M}
as define by (
5.5
) is a compact, positive and self-adjoint operator on
\mathbb{L}^{2}
. Therefore, the eigenfunctions of
\mathbb{M}:\nabla\mathrm{H}_{\textbf{arm}}\to\nabla\mathrm{H}_{\textbf{arm}}
form a complete orthonormal system in
\nabla\mathrm{H}_{\textbf{arm}}
. Let us consider
(\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}},\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}})_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}
be the orhonormal eigensystem of the magnetic operator in
\nabla\mathrm{H}_{\textbf{arm}}
. More details about the properties of magnetic operator can be found in
[
16
]
,
[
17
]
,
[
18
]
. Using this orthonormal eigensystem in (
5.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}-\alpha\Big\langle\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\alpha\Big\langle\mathbb{T}\Big[\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big];\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

(5.15)

With the self-adjointness of the magnetic operator, we evaluate the second term of the equation (
5.15
)

\displaystyle\Bigg\langle\nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}\ ;\ \mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H};\ \nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}d\mathrm{y}\Big\rangle_{\textbf{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H};\ \lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}.

(5.16)

Consequently, we obtain from the equations (
5.3
) and plugging it into the equation (
5.15
)

\displaystyle(1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}})\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\textbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\ =\ \Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\Big\langle\mathbb{T}\Big[\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big];\ \mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

(5.17)

Due to this, in order to estimate the error term associated with each equation arising from projection of the equation (
5.3
) onto the described sub-spaces we need an a priori estimate which we provide in the following proposition

Proposition 5.1.1

We have

\displaystyle\Big\|\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{1-\mathrm{h}}\Big)\quad\text{for}\quad 0<\mathrm{h}<1.

(5.18)

Proof.
See section
5.4
.

Based on these orthonormal bases we can arrive at a basis for the whole space
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)
. Consider the complete orthonormal basis
\Big\{\big(\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\big),\big(\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\big),\big(\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\big)\Big\}_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}.
In order to estimate
\nabla\mathrm{H}
, we use the Parseval’s identity

\displaystyle\Big\|\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}\\
\mathrm{i}=1,2,3\end{subarray}}\Big|\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H};\mathrm{e}^{(\mathrm{i})}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\Big|^{2}.

(5.19)

As a next step, we examine the equation (
5.17
) and rewrite it in the scaled domain
\mathrm{B}

\displaystyle\Big(1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big)\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}=\big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}+\alpha\delta^{2}\Big\langle\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big];\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}

(5.20)

and derive the estimate

\displaystyle\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|=\dfrac{1}{|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}|}\Bigg[\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|+|\alpha|\delta^{2}\Big|\Big\langle\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big];\ \mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|\Bigg].

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}\lesssim\dfrac{1}{|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}|^{2}}\Bigg[\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}+\delta^{4}\Big|\Big\langle\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big];\ \mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}\Bigg].

(5.21)

In a similar way, we obtain from (
5.14
) and (
5.11
)

\displaystyle\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|=\dfrac{1}{|1-\alpha|^{2}}\Bigg[\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|+|\alpha|\delta^{2}\Big|\Big\langle\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big];\ \mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|\Bigg].

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}\lesssim\dfrac{1}{|1-\alpha|^{2}}\Bigg[\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}+\delta^{4}\Big|\Big\langle\mathbb{T}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big];\ \mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}\Bigg],

(5.22)

and

\displaystyle\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}\lesssim\delta^{4}\Big|\Big\langle\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big];\ \mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}

(5.23)

respectively.

Furthermore, we rewrite the expression (
5.19
) as follows

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}|^{2}(\mathrm{\eta})d\mathrm{\eta}=\Big|\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H};\mathrm{e}^{(\mathrm{i})}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}\big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{n}\neq\mathrm{n}_{0}\\
\mathrm{i}=1,2,3\end{subarray}}\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(\mathrm{i})}_{\mathrm{n}}\big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}.

(5.24)

Let us recall that from the Lorentz model we can deduce the following, see Section
7.4.1
for details,

\displaystyle\big|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big|=\begin{cases}\delta^{\mathrm{h}}&\mathrm{n}=\mathrm{n}_{0}\\
1&\mathrm{n}\neq\mathrm{n}_{0}.\end{cases}

(5.25)

Therefore, considering the estimates (
5.3
), (
5.3
), and (
5.23
) and plugging these results into the previous series expansion (
5.24
) we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}|^{2}(\mathrm{\eta})d\mathrm{\eta}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}|^{2}}\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};e^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}+\dfrac{1}{|1-\alpha|^{2}}\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};e^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{n}\neq\mathrm{n}_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(\mathrm{3})}_{\mathrm{n}}\big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}}{|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}|^{2}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{n}\neq\mathrm{n}_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(\mathrm{2})}_{\mathrm{n}}\big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}}{|1-\alpha|^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\Big(\dfrac{1}{|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}|^{2}}\delta^{4}\Big\|\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big]\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big).

(5.26)

As a next step, we deal with the second term of the previous expression (
5.3
) i.e.
\Big\langle\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}};e^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.
We have the following lemma

Lemma 5.2

The eigen-function
\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}(\cdot)
satisfies the following mean vanishing integral property

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}(\mathrm{x)}d\mathrm{x}=0.

(5.27)

Since,
\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}}
is smooth, we can use Taylor’s series expansion, and hence we observe that the dominating term is related to
\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}(\mathrm{x)}d\mathrm{x}
, which is zero by the Lemma
5.2
. Thus we deduce that

\displaystyle\Bigg|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Bigg|^{2}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{2}\Big).

(5.28)

Taylor’s expansion provide

\displaystyle\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}=\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{z})\int_{\mathrm{B}}\tilde{\mathrm{e}}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}+\mathcal{O}(\delta).

(5.29)

Also, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{n}\neq\mathrm{n}_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(\mathrm{3})}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}}{|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}|^{2}}\sim 1.

(5.30)

Combining three above estimates (
5.28
), (
5.29
), (
5.30
) and plugging this into (
5.3
) we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}=\dfrac{1}{|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}|^{2}}\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};e^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}+\mathcal{O}\Big(1\Big).

(5.31)

Consequently, from the fact
|\nabla\mathrm{H}|^{2}=|\mathrm{E}|^{2}
and when we scale back to
\Omega
, we derive that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}=\dfrac{1}{|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}|^{2}}\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};e^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}\delta^{2}+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{2}\Big).

(5.32)

Moreover, after plugging the estimate (
5.29
) in the above expression, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}=\dfrac{1}{|1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}|^{2}}\Bigg[|\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}}|^{2}(\mathrm{z})\Big(\int_{\mathrm{B}}\tilde{\mathrm{e}}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}\Big)^{2}\delta^{2}+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{3}\Big)\Bigg]\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{h}<1.

(5.33)

This completes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

5.4
A Priori Estimate

This section is divided into two subsections providing the proof for Proposition
5.1.1
and Proposition
4.2.1
respectively.

5.4.1
Proof of Proposition

5.1.1

We need to identify the exact dominating term in the equations (
5.11
), (
5.14
), and (
5.17
). We start with the equation

\displaystyle\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{x})-\alpha\ \nabla\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}=\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{x})+\alpha\mathbb{T}\Big[\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big].

(5.34)

It can be showed that from the definition (
5.6
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}\Big[\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big]\approx\delta^{2}\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big]

(5.35)

therefore, after rewriting the equation (
5.34
) in the scaled domain
\mathrm{B}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}-\alpha\ \nabla\int_{\mathrm{B}}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{\xi},\mathrm{\eta})\cdot\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}(\mathrm{\eta})d\mathrm{\eta}=\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}}+\alpha\delta^{2}\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big].

(5.36)

Now, we estimate
\nabla\mathrm{H}
on the sub-space
\nabla\mathrm{H}_{\textbf{arm}}
.
Taking the induced
\textbf{L}^{2}
-norm for the equation (
5.34
), we get

\displaystyle\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}-\alpha\Big\langle\nabla\int_{\mathrm{B}}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{\xi},\mathrm{\eta})\cdot\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}(\mathrm{\eta})d\mathrm{\eta};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}=\big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}+\alpha\delta^{2}\Big\langle\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big];\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}.

(5.37)

Then due to the self-adjointness of the magnetization operator
\mathbb{M}
and the orthonormal system
\Big(\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}},\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big(1-\alpha\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big)\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}=\big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}+\alpha\delta^{2}\Big\langle\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big];\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}

(5.38)

and then

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}=\delta^{-2\mathrm{h}}\sum_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}+|\alpha|^{2}\delta^{4-2\mathrm{h}}\sum_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}\Big|\Big\langle\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big];\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}.

(5.39)

In a similar way as above, if we consider the orhonormal systems
\Big(\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big)
and
\Big(\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big)
, we deduce from (
5.36
)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}=|\alpha|^{2}\delta^{4}\sum_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}\Big|\Big\langle\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big];\mathrm{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}

(5.40)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}=\frac{1}{|1-\alpha|^{2}}\sum_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}\Big|\Big\langle\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}};\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2}+\frac{|\alpha|^{2}\delta^{4}}{|1-\alpha|^{2}}\sum_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}\Big|\Big\langle\mathbb{T}_{\delta}\Big[\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big];\mathrm{e}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{n}}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big|^{2},

(5.41)

respectively.

Therefore, combining the expressions (
5.39
), (
5.40
), (
5.41
) and due to the boundedness of the operator
\mathbb{T}:\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})\to\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathrm{B})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\lesssim\delta^{-2\mathrm{h}}\Big\|\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}}\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}+\delta^{4-2\mathrm{h}}\Big\|\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}

(5.42)

hence

\displaystyle\Big\|\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\lesssim\delta^{-2\mathrm{h}}\Big\|\widehat{\nabla\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}}\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}.

(5.43)

Moreover, as the incident wave is smooth enough, so we deduce

\Big\|\nabla\mathrm{H}^{\text{in}}\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2}).

(5.44)

Combining (
5.43
) and (
5.44
), we get

\displaystyle\Big\|\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}^{2}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{2-2h}\Big)\ \text{with the condition}\ h<1.

(5.45)

This completes the proof of Proposition
5.1.1
.

Next, we want to make a short remark, which will be essential to describe the proof of Theorem
1.3
. As
|\nabla\mathrm{H}|^{2}=|\mathrm{E}|^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|E\Big\|_{{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{1-h}\Big)\quad\text{with the condition}\quad\mathrm{h}<1.

(5.46)

5.4.2
Proof of Proposition
4.2.1

Let us state the following lemma before proceeding to give the proof of Proposition
4.2.1
.

Lemma 5.3

Suppose
0<\delta\leq 1
and
\Omega=\delta\mathrm{B}+x^{*}.
Then for
\nabla\varphi\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}=\|\nabla\hat{\varphi}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}(\Omega)}=\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla\hat{\varphi}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}(\mathrm{B})}.

(5.47)

Proof.
Let us suppose
\mathrm{x}=\delta\xi+\mathrm{z}
. Then we obtain for
\nabla\varphi\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\varphi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\varphi(\mathrm{x})|^{2}d\mathrm{x}

\displaystyle=\delta^{2}\int_{\mathrm{B}}\delta^{-2}|\nabla_{\xi}\varphi(\delta\xi+z)|^{2}d\xi

\displaystyle=\|\nabla\hat{\varphi}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}

(5.48)

and for
\nabla\varphi\in\mathbb{L}^{4}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\varphi\|^{4}_{\mathbb{L}^{4}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\varphi(\mathrm{x})|^{4}d\mathrm{x}

\displaystyle=\delta^{2}\int_{\mathrm{B}}\delta^{-4}|\nabla_{\xi}\varphi(\delta\xi+z)|^{4}d\xi

\displaystyle=\delta^{-2}\|\nabla\hat{\varphi}\|^{4}_{\mathbb{L}^{4}(\mathrm{B})},

(5.49)

which leads to (
5.47
).

Next, we start with the Lippmann-Schwinger equation in order to estimate
\Big\||\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{x})-\alpha\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}=\mathrm{H}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{x})+\alpha\int_{\Omega}\nabla\Big(\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}-\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\Big)(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}.

(5.50)

From the asymptotic expression of the Hankel function as
|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|\to 0
, we obtain the expansions:

\displaystyle\Big(\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}-\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\Big)(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})=a|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}\log{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}+b|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}+\mathcal{O}(|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{3}),

(5.51)

and

\displaystyle\nabla\Big(\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}-\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\Big)(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})=\mathrm{C}|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|\log{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}+\mathrm{D}|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|+\mathcal{O}(|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}),

(5.52)

as
|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|\to 0
with some constants where
\mathrm{a}
and
\mathrm{b}
,
\mathrm{C}
and
\mathrm{D}
. Then, we write the above expression in the domain
\mathrm{B}
i.e. after scaling we obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\xi)-\alpha\int_{\mathrm{B}}\nabla_{\eta}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\xi,\eta)\cdot\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathrm{H}}^{in}(\xi)+\alpha\delta^{2}\Big[\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\log{|\xi-\eta|}\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle+\log{\delta}\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta+\delta\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta\Big].

(5.53)

The Newtonian operator
\mathcal{V}:\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})\to\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathrm{B})
is continuous and then we have the continuity of
\nabla\cdot\mathcal{V}:\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})\to\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B})
.
Consequently, since we have
\|\nabla\mathrm{H}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}=\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{1-h}\Big)
and taking
\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B})
-norm of the equation (
5.4.2
), we derive

\displaystyle\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B})}+|\alpha|\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}+|\alpha|\delta^{2}\Big[\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}+|\log{\delta}|\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}+\delta\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Big(1\Big)+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{1-\mathrm{h}})+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{3-\mathrm{h}}\Big)+\mathcal{O}\Big(|\log{\delta}|\delta^{3-\mathrm{h}}\Big)+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{4-\mathrm{h}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Big(1\Big).

(5.54)

We state the scaled equation (
5.4.2
)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\xi)-\alpha\int_{\mathrm{B}}\nabla_{\eta}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\xi,\eta)\cdot\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathrm{H}}^{in}(\xi)+\alpha\delta^{2}\Big[\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\log{|\xi-\eta|}\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle+\log{\delta}\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta+\delta\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta\Big]

(5.55)

and rewrite it as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\xi)+\alpha\int_{\partial\mathrm{B}}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\xi,\eta)\partial_{\nu_{\eta}}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta+\alpha\delta^{2}\delta_{c}\mu_{m}\omega^{2}\int_{\mathrm{B}}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\xi,\eta)\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathrm{H}}^{in}(\xi)+\alpha\delta^{2}\Big[\delta\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)

\displaystyle+\log{\delta}\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\log{|\xi-\eta|}\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta d\eta\Big].

(5.56)

Taking the normal derivative, as
\xi\to\partial\mathrm{B}
from inside
\mathrm{B}
, from the jump relations of the derivative of the single layer potential we obtain

\displaystyle\Big(I+\frac{\alpha}{2}\Big)\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}

\displaystyle=-\alpha\mathbb{K}^{*}_{0}\Big[\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big]-\alpha\delta^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}\mu_{m}\omega^{2}\partial_{\nu}\int_{\mathrm{B}}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\xi,\eta)\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta+\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}^{in}+\alpha\delta^{2}\Big[\delta\partial_{\nu}\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle+\partial_{\nu}\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\ln{|\xi-\eta|}\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta+\log{\delta}\partial_{\nu}\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta\Big]

(5.57)

then

\displaystyle\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha}{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\mathbb{K}^{*}_{0}\Big[\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big]+\frac{1}{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}^{in}+\frac{\alpha\delta^{2}}{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\Bigg[-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\omega^{2}\partial_{\nu}\int_{\mathrm{B}}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\xi,\eta)\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle+\partial_{\nu}\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\ln{|\xi-\eta|}\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta+\log{\delta}\partial_{\nu}\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta+\delta\partial_{\nu}\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta\Bigg].

(5.58)

Next, we state the following lemma, for details we refer to
[
8
, Theorem 3.6, p. 43]
.

Lemma 5.4

If
\mathrm{B}
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{2}
, then we have the following regularity

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\mathbb{K}^{*}_{0}\Big[\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\mathrm{B})}\lesssim\Big\|\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\mathrm{B})}.

(5.59)

Also, for
\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})
, we have
\displaystyle\int_{B}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\xi,\eta)\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathrm{B})
and then
\displaystyle\partial_{\nu}\int_{\mathrm{B}}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\xi,\eta)\tilde{\mathrm{H}}(\eta)d\eta\in\mathbb{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathrm{B})
. Thus, after taking the
\mathbb{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathrm{B})
-norm on both side of the equation (
5.4.2
), observing the fact
\Big|\frac{\alpha}{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\Big|\sim 1
, and since the integrals on the right hand side of the expression (
5.4.2
) are also bounded, we deduce the estimate

\displaystyle\Big\|\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\mathrm{B})}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\Big\|\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\mathrm{B})}+\Big\|\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\mathrm{B})}+\delta^{2}\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}+\delta^{2}\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}+\delta^{2}|\log{\delta}|\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}

\displaystyle+\delta^{3}\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Bigg).

(5.60)

Consequently,

\displaystyle\Big\|\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\mathrm{B})}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B})}+\Big\|\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\textbf{in}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\mathrm{B})}+\delta^{2}\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}+\delta^{2}\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}+\delta^{2}|\log{\delta}|\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}

\displaystyle+\delta^{3}\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathrm{B})}\Bigg).

(5.61)

Hence, based on the estimate (
5.4.2
),
\Big\|\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}=\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{1-h}\Big)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\mathrm{B})}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Big(1\Big)+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{2}\Big)+\mathcal{O}\Big(|\log{\delta}|\delta^{3-h}\Big)+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{3-h}\Big)+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{4-h}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Big(1\Big).

(5.62)

Moreover, from the Sobolev embedding inequality and considering the well-posed problem satisfied by
\tilde{\mathrm{H}}
, we deduce

\displaystyle\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}(\mathrm{B})}\lesssim\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}}(\mathrm{B})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big\|\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\mathrm{B})}+\delta^{2}\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\partial\mathrm{B})}=\mathcal{O}\Big(1\Big).

(5.63)

Hence, by Lemma
5.3
, when we scale back to
\Omega
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|\nabla\mathrm{H}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|\nabla\tilde{\mathrm{H}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}(\mathrm{B})}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big).

(5.64)

Consequently, from the above estimate and due to the fact
|\mathrm{E}|^{2}=|\nabla\mathrm{H}|^{2}
we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\||\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{-1}\Big).

(5.65)

This completes the proof of Proposition
4.2.1
.

6
Proof of Theorem
1.2

We recall the related model

\displaystyle\Delta\mathrm{E}+\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\varepsilon\mathrm{E}=0

(6.1)

where the electric permittivity
\varepsilon={\varepsilon_{p}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\Omega}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}}
,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}=\varepsilon_{\infty}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\mathrm{m}}
is the permittivity of the host medium and
\mu_{\mathrm{m}}=\mu_{\infty}\mu^{\prime}_{\mathrm{m}}
is the permeability of the host medium respectively, both are assumed to be constant, independent of the frequency
\omega
of the incident wave. Here,
\varepsilon_{\infty}
and
\mu_{\infty}
as the permittivity and permeability of the vacuum. For the permittivity,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}(\omega)
of the Dielectric nanoparticle, we will use the so-called Lorenz model for the permittivity, which can be described as follows

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}(\omega)=\varepsilon_{\infty}\Bigg[1+\dfrac{\omega_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}}{\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2}-i\gamma\omega}\Bigg]

(6.2)

where where
\omega^{2}_{\mathrm{p}}
is the electric plasma frequency,
\omega^{2}_{0}
is the undamped resonance frequency and
\gamma
is the electric damping parameter.
Furthermore, let us recall that the volumetric Logarithmic Operator
\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}
has eigenvalues
\lambda_{\mathrm{n}}^{(\bm{\ell})}
and the corresponding eigenfunctions
\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{n}}^{(\bm{\ell})}
. The following properties are needed, namely, for a particle
\Omega
of radius
\delta<<1
,
\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{n}}^{(\bm{\ell})}(\mathrm{x})d\mathrm{x}\neq 0
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{n}}^{(\bm{\ell})}\sim\delta^{2}|\log\delta|.
These properties are are justified for
n=1
, when
\Omega
is the disc of radius
\delta
, see
[
20
]
.

The unique solution of the problem (
6.1
) satisfies also the following Lippmann-Schwinger equation

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{x})-\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\Big(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}(\omega,\gamma)-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}\Big)\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}=\mathrm{E}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{x})

(6.3)

where we recall the fundamental solution,
\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}

\mathbb{G}^{(k)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})=\dfrac{i}{4}\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{0}(k|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|),\quad\textbf{for}\quad\mathrm{x}\neq\mathrm{y}.

Moreover, we denote by
\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}:=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}(\omega,\gamma)-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}
and we also use the fact that
|1-\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}|\sim|\log\delta|^{-\mathrm{h}}
, see Section
6
. The equation (
6.4
) can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{x})-\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{0})}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}=\mathrm{E}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{x})+\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}\int_{\Omega}\Big(\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}-\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{0})}\Big)(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}.

(6.4)

Using the asymptotic expression
|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|\to 0

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}^{(k)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})-\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})=\bm{a}|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}\ln{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}+\bm{b}|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}+\mathcal{O}(|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{3})

(6.5)

in the equation (
6.4
), rewritten in scaled domain
\mathrm{B}
with
|\mathrm{B}|=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\Omega=\delta\mathrm{B}+\mathrm{z}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{\xi})-\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}\delta^{2}\int_{\mathrm{B}}\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{0})}(\mathrm{\xi},\mathrm{\eta})\tilde{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{\eta})d\mathrm{\eta}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathrm{E}}^{\textbf{in}}(\mathrm{\xi})-\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}\delta^{2}\Bigg[\log\delta\int_{\mathrm{B}}\tilde{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{\eta})d\mathrm{\eta}+\delta^{2}\log\delta\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|^{2}\tilde{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{\eta})d\mathrm{\eta}

\displaystyle+\delta^{2}\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|^{2}\ln|\xi-\eta|\tilde{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{\eta})d\mathrm{\eta}+\delta^{2}\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|^{2}\tilde{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{\eta})d\mathrm{\eta}

\displaystyle+\delta^{3}\int_{\mathrm{B}}|\xi-\eta|^{3}\tilde{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{\eta})d\mathrm{\eta}\Bigg].

(6.6)

Moreover, we use the local
\mathrm{H}^{2}
-regularity of the solution of (
6.1
) in
\Omega
. The following inequality is a consequence of interpolation inequalities based on Gagliardo-Nirenberg, we refer to
[
7
, p. 313]
.

Lemma 6.1

Let
\mathrm{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{N}}
be a regular bounded open set. Let
1\leq\mathrm{q}\leq\mathrm{p}<\infty
. Then the following inequality follows

\displaystyle\Big\|u\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\mathrm{p}}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)}\lesssim\Big\|u\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\mathrm{q}}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)}^{1-\mathrm{r}}\Big\|u\Big\|_{W^{1,\mathrm{N}}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)}^{\mathrm{r}}\quad\forall u\in W^{1,\mathrm{N}}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big),\quad\textbf{where}\quad\mathrm{r}=1-\frac{\mathrm{q}}{\mathrm{p}}

(6.7)

In particular, for
\mathrm{N}=2,\mathrm{p}=4,\mathrm{q}=2
and
\mathrm{r}=\frac{1}{2}
we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|u\Big\|_{L^{\mathrm{4}}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)}\lesssim\Big\|u\Big\|_{L^{\mathrm{2}}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|u\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.8)

Next, we use the continuity property of the integral
\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{B}}\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{0})}(\mathrm{\xi},\mathrm{\eta})\tilde{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{\eta})d\mathrm{\eta}
from
\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)\to\mathrm{H}^{2}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)
and the smoothness of the remaining integral of the equation (
6
) to obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{E}}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{2}\Big(B\Big)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{E}}^{\textbf{in}}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{2}\Big(B\Big)}+\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}\delta^{2}\Bigg[\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{E}}\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)}+|\ln\delta|\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{E}}\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)}+\delta^{2}|\ln{\delta}|\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{E}}\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)}+\delta^{2}\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{E}}\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)}

\displaystyle+\delta^{3}\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{E}}\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\mathrm{B}\Big)}\Bigg].

(6.9)

We have the following estimate as derived in
[
20
]
,

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathrm{E}\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta|\log{\delta}|^{\mathrm{h}}\Big).

(6.10)

Therefore with the help of (
6.10
), we derive that

\displaystyle\Big\|\tilde{\mathrm{E}}\Big\|_{\mathrm{H}^{2}\Big(B\Big)}=\mathcal{O}\Big(1\Big).

(6.11)

Hence by interpolation, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\||\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\Big(\Omega\Big)}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta|\log\delta|^{\frac{3\mathrm{h}}{2}}\Big).

(6.12)

Next, we state the following result, obtained in
[
20
]
, to derive the dominant term of
\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}
.

Lemma 6.2

We have the following approximation

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}=\dfrac{|\mathrm{E}^{\textbf{in}}|^{2}(\mathrm{z)}\Big(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}\Big)^{2}}{|1-\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}|^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{2}|\log\delta|^{3\mathrm{h}-1}\Big).

(6.13)

7
Appendix

7.1
Derivation of the System of Boundary Integral Equations:

As a first step, we rewrite the representation (
2
) as follows

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}(\mathrm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)-\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big](\mathrm{x},t),\ \text{for}\ \mathrm{x}\in\Omega\times(0,\mathrm{T}).

(7.1)

By using the jump relation of the heat potential we can obtain the Neumann trace of the (
7.1
)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}(\mathrm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)-\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{1}{2}+\mathcal{K^{*}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)-\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big](\mathrm{x},t),

where
\mathcal{K^{*}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}}
is the adjoint of double layer heat operator. Next, we denote
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}
as the hypersingular boundary integral operator with density
\psi

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\psi\Big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}):=-\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\psi\Big](\mathrm{x},t)=-\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\partial\Omega}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{y}}\Phi(\mathrm{x},t;\mathrm{y},\tau)\psi(y,\tau)d\sigma_{\mathrm{y}}d\tau.

(7.2)

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}-\mathcal{K}^{*}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Bigg)\Bigg[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Bigg](\mathrm{x},t)=\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big](\mathrm{x},t).

(7.3)

In a similar way, taking the dirichlet trace of the equation (
7.1
) and using the jump relation of heat potential, we get

\displaystyle\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0,\mathrm{x}}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}(\mathrm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)-\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0,\mathrm{x}}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)-\Big(-\frac{1}{2}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big](\mathrm{x},t),

where
\mathcal{K}
is the double layer heat operator. Hence, we get

\displaystyle\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Bigg)\Bigg[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Bigg](\mathrm{x},t)=\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big](\mathrm{x},t).

(7.4)

Thus, using the internal Dirichlet problem along with the initial condition, we arrive at the integral representations

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)=\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big](\mathrm{x},t),

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}^{*}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)=\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1,\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big](\mathrm{x},t).

(7.5)

In a similar manner, for exterior Dirichlet Problem we arrive to the integral representations

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)=-\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t),

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}^{*}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)=-\mathcal{H}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t),

(7.6)

where
\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}=\frac{\rho_{m}C_{m}}{\gamma_{m}}
. From the first boundary-integral equation of (
7.1
), we can have

\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}=-\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}^{-1}\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}\Big].

(7.7)

Also, from the second boundary-integral equation of (
7.1
), we have

\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}=-\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)+\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}^{*}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t).

(7.8)

Now replacing (
7.7
) in (
7.8
), we get

\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}=-\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\Big[\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}\Big](\mathrm{x},t)-\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}^{*}\Big)\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}^{-1}\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}\Big].

(7.9)

We denote by
\mathbb{A}^{\textbf{ext}}:=\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}+\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}^{*}\Big)\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}^{-1}\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\Big)
, which is known as Steklov-Poincare heat operator.
Again from the first boundary-integral equation of (
7.1
), we get

\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}=\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}^{-1}\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big]-\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}^{-1}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big](\mathrm{x},t).

(7.10)

Therefore, from the transmission condition of (
1.8
) and replacing the equations (
7.9
) and (
7.10
) in that, we get

\displaystyle\gamma_{c}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}=\gamma_{m}\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{1}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}

\displaystyle\gamma_{c}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}^{-1}\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big]-\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}^{-1}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big]=-\gamma_{m}\mathbb{A}^{\textbf{ext}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}\Big]

(7.11)

Now from the transmission condition
\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}=\gamma^{\textbf{ext}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{e}}
, we obtain from the equation (
7.1
)

\displaystyle\gamma_{c}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}^{-1}\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big]=-\gamma_{m}\mathbb{A}^{\textbf{ext}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big]+\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\gamma_{c}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}^{-1}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big],

\displaystyle\text{equivalently,}\ \ \ \gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}=-\frac{\gamma_{\mathrm{m}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\mathbb{A}^{\textbf{ext}}\Big[\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\Big]+\frac{\omega\cdot\bm{\Im}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}})}{2\pi\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}I_{d}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)^{-1}\gamma^{\textbf{int}}_{0}\mathcal{V}\Big[|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\Big].

(7.12)

7.2
Proof of Lemma
4.1

We start with recalling the definition of the fundamental solution for heat equation

\Phi(\mathrm{y},\tau;\mathrm{v},\mathrm{s}):=\ \begin{cases}\dfrac{\alpha}{4\pi(\tau-\mathrm{s})}\textbf{exp}\Big(-\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4(\tau-\mathrm{s})}\Big),\quad\tau>\mathrm{s}\\
0,\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

(7.13)

where we set
\alpha:=\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}=\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{p}}C_{\mathrm{p}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{p}}}
, and the corresponding double layer heat operator
\mathcal{K}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}[f](\mathrm{y},\tau):=\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\partial\Omega}\partial_{\nu_{\mathrm{y}}}\Phi(\mathrm{y},\tau;\mathrm{v},\mathrm{s})f(\mathrm{v},\mathrm{s})d\sigma_{\mathrm{v}}d\mathrm{s}.

(7.14)

We see that
\partial_{\nu_{\mathrm{y}}}\Phi(\mathrm{y},\tau;\mathrm{v},\mathrm{s}):=-\frac{\alpha(\mathrm{v}-\mathrm{y})\cdot\nu_{\mathrm{y}}}{2(\tau-\mathrm{s})}\Phi(\mathrm{y},\tau;\mathrm{v},\mathrm{s}).

Furthermore, we recall the fundamental solution corresponding to the Laplace equation
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{y},\mathrm{v}):=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln(|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|)
and then
\partial_{\nu_{\mathrm{y}}}\mathbb{G}^{(0)}(\mathrm{y},\mathrm{v}):=-\frac{(\mathrm{v}-\mathrm{y})\cdot\nu_{\mathrm{y}}}{2\pi}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}.

Hence, we derive the following representation from (
7.14
) applied to
f:=f_{\xi,\mathrm{t},\mathrm{z}}(\mathrm{y},\mathrm{\tau})=\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)
, which is constant with respect to the variable
\mathrm{y}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\Big[\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\Big](\mathrm{y},\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\partial\Omega}-\frac{\alpha(\mathrm{v}-\mathrm{y})\cdot\nu_{\mathrm{y}}}{2(\tau-\mathrm{s})}\Phi(\mathrm{y},\tau;\mathrm{v},\mathrm{s})\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\mathrm{s})d\sigma_{\mathrm{v}}d\mathrm{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\partial\Omega}-\frac{\alpha(\mathrm{v}-\mathrm{y})\cdot\nu_{\mathrm{y}}}{2(\tau-\mathrm{s})}\dfrac{\alpha}{4\pi(\tau-\mathrm{s})}\textbf{exp}\Big(-\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4(\tau-\mathrm{s})}\Big)\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\mathrm{s})d\sigma_{\mathrm{v}}d\mathrm{s}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}-\dfrac{(\mathrm{v}-\mathrm{y})\cdot\nu_{\mathrm{y}}}{2\pi}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}\Bigg[\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4(\tau-\mathrm{s})^{2}}\textbf{exp}\Big(-\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4(\tau-\mathrm{s})}\Big)\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\mathrm{s})d\mathrm{s}\Bigg]d\sigma_{\mathrm{v}}

(7.15)

Now, let us define

\varphi(\mathrm{v},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t},\tau):=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4(\mathrm{s}-\tau)^{2}}\textbf{exp}\Big(-\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4(\mathrm{s}-\tau)}\Big)\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\mathrm{s})d\mathrm{s}.

(7.16)

We use the change of variable
\mathrm{m}:=\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau-\mathrm{s}}}
, then it follows that

\mathrm{s}=\tau-\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}
and then
d\mathrm{s}=\frac{1}{2}\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}\mathrm{m}^{-3}.

Consequently, we obtain from (
7.16
)

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathrm{v},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t},\tau)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau}}}^{\infty}\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau-\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\Big)\frac{1}{(\tau-\mathrm{s})}\mathrm{m}^{2}\frac{1}{2}\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}\mathrm{m}^{-3}d\mathrm{m}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau}}}^{\infty}\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau-\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\Big)\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{2(\tau-\mathrm{s})}\mathrm{m}^{-1}d\mathrm{m}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau}}}^{\infty}\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau-\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\Big)2\mathrm{m}^{2}\mathrm{m}^{-1}d\mathrm{m}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}}^{\infty}\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau-\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\Big)d(\mathrm{m}^{2}).

(7.17)

We rewrite (
7.2
) as follows

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathrm{v},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t},\tau)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}}^{\infty}\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})\Bigg[\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau-\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\Big)-\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau\Big)\Bigg]d(\mathrm{m}^{2})

\displaystyle+\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau\Big)\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}}^{\infty}\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})d(\mathrm{m}^{2}).

(7.18)

From the identity
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})d(\mathrm{m}^{2})=1
, we derive the following

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathrm{v},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t},\tau)-\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}}^{\infty}\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})\Bigg[\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau-\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\Big)-\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau\Big)\Bigg]d(\mathrm{m}^{2})

\displaystyle-\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau)\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}}\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})d(\mathrm{m}^{2}).

(7.19)

Next, we do the following computation

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau)=\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{t}}^{\tau}\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\mathrm{s})d\mathrm{s}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|1\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{4}}(\mathrm{t},\tau)}\|\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(\mathrm{t},\tau)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\tau^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(0,\tau)}.

(7.20)

In a similar way as before, we write the following expression

\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau-\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\Big)-\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau\Big)=\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{\tau-\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}}\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\mathrm{s}\Big)d\mathrm{s}.

(7.21)

We observe that
\delta\geq\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau}}
which implies
\tau-\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\geq 0
.
Hence we get

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau-\dfrac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\Big)-\Phi^{\textbf{e}}\Big(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau\Big)=\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\mathrm{m}}\|\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(0,\tau)}\Big).

(7.22)

Therefore, we plug (
7.2
) and (
7.22
) in (
7.2
) to obtain

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathrm{v},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t},\tau)-\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}}^{\infty}\frac{\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})}{\mathrm{m}}d(\mathrm{m}^{2})\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2}\|\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(0,\tau)}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}}\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})d(\mathrm{m}^{2})\tau^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(0,\tau)}\Bigg).

(7.23)

Now, we look into the integral
\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}}^{\infty}\frac{\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})}{\mathrm{m}}d(\mathrm{m}^{2})
and substitute
\omega=\mathrm{m}^{2}
, then we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4t}}^{\infty}\omega^{\frac{1}{2}-1}\textbf{exp}(-\omega)d\omega=\Gamma\Big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}\Big).

Moreover, it is well known that
\Gamma\Big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}\Big)=\sqrt{\pi}\ \textbf{erfc}\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau}}\Big)
, where
\textbf{erfc}(\mathrm{z})
is the complementary error function and
\textbf{erfc}(\mathrm{z})=1-\textbf{erf}(\mathrm{z}).
The Taylor series expansion

\textbf{erf}(\mathrm{z})={\sqrt{\pi}}\Big(\mathrm{z}-\frac{\mathrm{z}^{3}}{3}+\frac{\mathrm{z}^{5}}{10}-...\Big)
, which holds for every
\mathrm{z}\in\mathbb{C}
, implies that

\displaystyle\Gamma\Big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}\Big)=\sqrt{\pi}\ \textbf{erfc}\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau}}\Big)=\sqrt{\pi}\Bigg(1-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Big(\mathrm{\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau}}}-\frac{\mathrm{(\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau}}})^{3}}{3}+\frac{\mathrm{(\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau}}})^{5}}{10}-...\Big)\Bigg).

(7.24)

We also note that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}}\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})d(\mathrm{m}^{2})=1-\textbf{exp}\Big(\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}\Big)=1-\Big[1-\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}+\frac{1}{2!}\Big(\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}\Big)^{2}-...\Big].

(7.25)

As a next step, we plug (
7.24
) and (
7.25
) into (
7.2
) to derive

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathrm{v},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t},\tau)-\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\sqrt{\pi}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2}\|\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(0,\tau)}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{\sqrt{\tau}}\|\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(0,\tau)}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\frac{\alpha|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|^{2}}{4\tau}\tau^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(0,\tau)}\Bigg),

(7.26)

and then we obtain

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathrm{v},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t},\tau)-\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\sqrt{\pi}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2}\|\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(0,\tau)}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau}}\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|\|\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(0,\tau)}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau}}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2}\|\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(0,\tau)}\Bigg).

(7.27)

Consequently, because of the boundness of the term
\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{\tau}}
we deduce that

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathrm{v},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t},\tau)-\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\tau)=\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|\ \|\partial_{s}\Phi^{\textbf{e}}(\xi,\mathrm{t};\mathrm{z},\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(0,\tau)}\Bigg).

(7.28)

This completes the proof.

7.3
Proof of Lemma
5.1

The goal is to show that the operator defined thought the expression

\mathbb{T}\Big[\mathrm{H}\Big](\mathrm{x})=\int_{\Omega}\nabla\nabla\cdot\Big(\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}-\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\Big)(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{y})d\mathrm{y}.

(7.29)

is a bounded operator from
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{2}(\Omega).

From the definition of Hankel function, we have the following asymptotic expansion

\partial_{\mathrm{ij}}\Big(\mathbb{G}^{(\mathrm{k})}-\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\Big)(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})=2a\delta_{\mathrm{ij}}\log|x-y|+2\mathrm{a}\dfrac{(\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y})_{\mathrm{i}}(\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y})_{\mathrm{j}}}{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}+2\mathrm{b}\delta_{\mathrm{ij}}+\mathcal{O}(|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|),\quad\mathrm{x}\sim\mathrm{y},

(7.30)

where
\delta_{\mathrm{ij}}
is the Kronecker delta. Here a and b are constants.

We see that the leading term of the expansion (
7.30
), when injected into (
7.29
), is the kernel of the volume potential operator
\mathcal{V}
which is bounded from
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)
to
\mathbb{H}^{2}(\Omega)
. The other terms of the expansion (
7.30
) define smoother kernels. Therefore they define smoother operators as compared to
\mathcal{V}
. This implies that the operator
\mathbb{T}
is bounded
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)
to
\mathbb{H}^{2}(\Omega)
.

7.4
Modelling the Electric Permittivity for Lorentzian Particles

The Lorentz model (also known as Drude-Lorentz oscillator model) describes an electron as damped harmonic oscillator driven by a strong force.
The purpose of this model is to determine, using Newton’s Second Law, the motion of an electron of the particle (and its polarization as it is proportional to it) when it comes in contact with the incidence electric field. The polarization moment satisfies the following equation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}P}{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{t}^{2}}+\gamma\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{t}}+\omega_{0}^{2}P=\varepsilon_{\infty}\omega^{2}_{p}u^{\mathrm{i}}

(7.31)

where
\omega_{0}^{2}
is the undamped resonance frequency,
\gamma
is the electric damping parameter,
\omega_{p}
the electric plasmonic frequency and
\varepsilon_{\infty}
is the permittivity of the vacuum. The above equation provides a basis for determining the polarization and the dielectric susceptibility (i.e. the equivalent dielectric constant). Indeed, consider the driving force to be an oscillating incidence electric field, which is given by the time-harmonic acoustic plane wave
u^{\mathrm{i}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}):=\mathrm{e}^{i(\mathrm{k}\mathrm{x}\cdot\mathrm{d}-\omega\mathrm{t})}

where,
\mathrm{k}=\frac{\omega}{\mathrm{c_{0}}}
is the wave number,
\omega
the frequency of incidence,
\mathrm{c_{0}}
the speed of light, in the vacuum, and
\mathrm{d}
the direction of incidence. We look for the polarization of the form
P(x,t):=P(x,\omega)e^{-i\omega t}
, then we obtain its expression, from (
7.31
), of the form
P(x,t)=\frac{\epsilon_{\infty}\omega_{p}}{-\omega^{2}-i\gamma\omega+\omega_{0}^{2}}u^{\mathrm{i}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}).

On the other hand, we know that the polarization is related to the incident field through the electric susceptibility
\chi_{e}
as
P(x,t)=\epsilon_{\infty}\chi_{e}u^{\mathrm{i}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})
. As the electric susceptibility is defined through the relation
\frac{\epsilon_{p}}{\epsilon_{\infty}}=1+\chi_{e}
, we derive the following expression of the permittivity
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}(\omega)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}(\omega)=\varepsilon_{\infty}\Bigg[1+\dfrac{\omega_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}}{\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2}-i\gamma\omega}\Bigg].

(7.32)

For more information and details about the model (
7.32
), we refer to
[
1
,
13
,
22
]
.

Based on this model, we discuss the regimes, in terms of the incident frequency
\omega
, where the nanoparticle behaves as a plasmonic nanoparticle or a as dielectric nanoparticle.

7.4.1
Modeling with Plasmonic Nanoparticles

The purpose of this section is to use the Lorentz model to generate the condition on the permittivity used in Section
4
, namely
|1-\alpha\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}|=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}}),
where
\alpha=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}(\omega)}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}}
and
\mathrm{h}<1
. Here,
\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}
’s are the eigenvalues of the Magnetization operator
\mathbb{M}:\nabla\mathrm{H}_{\text{arm}}\rightarrow\nabla\mathrm{H}_{\text{arm}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}(\omega),\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}
are the corresponding permittivity of the particle and host medium respectively. We state the following lemma.

Lemma 7.1

If we consider the electric damping parameter
\gamma\sim\delta^{\mathrm{h}}
and the frequency
\omega
of the incidence wave as
\omega^{2}=\omega_{0}^{2}+\omega_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}\dfrac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}}{\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}(1-\varepsilon_{\infty}^{-1}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}})+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}}+O(\delta^{\mathrm{h}})
, we have the required equality
|1-\alpha\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}|=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}}).

Before justifying this lemma, let us mention that under the conditions on
\gamma
and
\omega
, the permittivity of the nanoparticle has a negative real part (as
\omega^{2}>\omega_{0}^{2}
). This negativity property is the one that is usually used in the literature to design plasmonic nanoparticles. In our analysis, the choice of the incident frequency of the form presented in the previous lemma is key.

Proof.
From the definition of
\alpha
and the fact that both
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}
are moderate in terms of the parameter
\delta
, then we have

\displaystyle 1-\alpha\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}})\iff\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}-\dfrac{\Big(\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}\Big)^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}})

Then substituting the value of
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}
, derived from the Lorentz model, we deduce

\displaystyle 1-\alpha\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}})\iff\dfrac{\omega_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}}{\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2}-i\gamma\omega}=-1+\varepsilon_{\infty}^{-1}\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}-\dfrac{\varepsilon_{\infty}^{-1}(\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)})^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}}).

(7.33)

Now, let us denote
\beta:=-1+\varepsilon_{\infty}^{-1}\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}-\dfrac{\varepsilon_{\infty}^{-1}\Big(\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}\Big)^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}}
and considering
\gamma=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}})
we obtain

\displaystyle 1-\alpha\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}})\iff\omega^{2}=\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}\cdot\beta^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}}).

(7.34)

Consequently, with the calculation
\beta^{-1}=\dfrac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}}{\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}(\varepsilon_{\infty}^{-1}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}-1)-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}}
we derive the needed frequency to acquired the desired equality

\displaystyle 1-\alpha\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}})\iff\omega^{2}=\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}\dfrac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}}{\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(3)}(\varepsilon_{\infty}^{-1}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}-1)-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{m}}}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\mathrm{h}})

(7.35)

which completes the proof.

7.4.2
Modelling with Dielectric Nanoparticles

The purpose of this section is to show under which conditions, on the incident frequency
\omega
and the damping parameter
\gamma
, the nanoparticle behaves as a dielectric nanoparticle. Namely, the real part, and also the imaginary part, of its permittivity is large with a small ratio between the imaginary and the real parts. Such scales infer the nanoparticle to enjoy high contrast of the relative index of refraction and small relative absorption. These last properties are those usually used in the literature to describe the dielectric nanoparticles. In our analysis, we need precise choices of the incident frequency
\omega
and damping frequency
\gamma
.

Recall the eigenvalues
\overline{\lambda}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}
of the Newtonian operator stated on the nanoparticle
\Omega
in the 2D-case. We used the assumption that
\overline{\lambda}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}\sim\delta^{2}\log\delta
, with
\delta
as the diameter of the
\Omega
. This assumption is valid for
n_{0}=1
, i.e. the first eigenvalue. We also recall the contrast parameter
\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}=\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{\infty}}}-1
.

Lemma 7.2

If the frequency of the incidence wave
\omega
is chosen close to the undamped resonance frequency
\omega^{2}_{0}
with
\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2}\sim\delta^{2}|\log\delta|\Big(\overline{\lambda}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\omega_{0}^{2}\Big)
and
\gamma\omega\sim\delta^{2}|\log\delta|^{1-\mathrm{h}-\mathrm{s}}\Big(\overline{\lambda}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\omega_{0}^{2}\Big)^{2}
, then we obtain
\bm{\Re}\Big(\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)\sim\delta^{-2}|\ln{\delta}|^{-1}\Big(\overline{\lambda}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\omega_{0}^{2}\Big)^{-1}
and
\bm{\Im}\Big(\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)\sim\delta^{-2}|\log{\delta}|^{-1-\mathrm{h}-\mathrm{s}}\Big(\overline{\lambda}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\omega_{0}^{2}\Big)^{-1}
, where
\mathrm{h}<1
and
\mathrm{s}>0
.

With these choices, we see that
\frac{\omega^{2}}{\omega_{0}^{2}}\sim 1
. In addition, we derive the needed relation
|1-\omega^{2}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{p}}\lambda_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}|\sim|\log\delta|^{-\mathrm{h}}.

Proof.
From the Lorentz’s model, we see that

\displaystyle\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}

\displaystyle\sim\dfrac{\omega_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}}{\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2}-i\gamma\omega}

\displaystyle\sim\dfrac{\omega_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}(\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2})}{(\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2})^{2}+(\gamma\omega)^{2}}+i\dfrac{\gamma\omega}{(\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2})^{2}+(\gamma\omega)^{2}}

Furthermore, as we assume
\gamma\omega=o\Big(\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2}\Big)
, then we obtain

\displaystyle\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\omega_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}}{\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2}}+i\frac{\gamma\omega}{(\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2})^{2}}.

(7.36)

Therefore, we derive the scales

\bm{\Re}\Big(\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)\sim\delta^{-2}|\ln{\delta}|^{-1}\Big(\overline{\lambda}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\omega_{0}^{2}\Big)^{-1}\mbox{ and }~\bm{\Im}\Big(\bm{\tau_{\mathrm{p}}}\Big)\sim\delta^{-2}|\log{\delta}|^{-1-\mathrm{h}-\mathrm{s}}\Big(\overline{\lambda}_{\mathrm{n_{0}}}^{(\bm{\ell})}\mu_{\mathrm{m}}\omega_{0}^{2}\Big)^{-1},\mbox{ where }\mathrm{h}<1.
